# Decisions

A running log of architecture and design decisions: what was chosen, why, and what to revisit. Newest first.

## Template
- Date:
- Decision:
- Options considered:
- Why:
- Revisit if:

---

## Phase 1 decisions

Decided during the Phase 1 ingestion-client investigation, after probing the live SEC EDGAR APIs rather than trusting the Phase 0 brief's literal wording.

### D73: The memo page (third of three commits that turn a checked narrative into a memo)
- Date: 2026-11-23
- Decision: A memo is shown as one self-contained HTML page. `memo/render_page.py` turns D71's rendered memo, D72's provenance, a small header model and the record's stamp into one string, and `workflow/page.py` builds it from a record (`memo_page`), which renders the narrative again and so runs the checker again. In reading order the page has: the head (charset, viewport, the Content-Security-Policy, the title, the style); a header with the kicker, the company's name as the heading, the dek and the scope note and the AI disclosure as two paragraphs; one section for each part of the narrative that has claims, under its approved heading, with the exchange note first under Exchange status; a Terms section; a Sources section; and a footer. A fact claim is a paragraph and an inference a paragraph marked as a reading, which begins with the word Reading (the approved reading label without its colon) and a space, so that copied text and a screen reader keep the label apart from the sentence. The builder found the missing space and flagged it in its report. A term is set apart, an amount or a count is set in figures that line up, every other value is plain text, and each claim ends with its source marks, each run of numbers a link to the first source of the run. The look and the style were approved by Austin from a preview built from run 2's agmh.one draft, and the style is kept byte for byte in `memo/render_page_css.py`.
- The words. The statements, the section headings, the reading label and the source labels are D71's. The kicker ("ListingLens risk memo"), the title ("{company name}: ListingLens risk memo"), every sentence of the dek, the three footer lines, the headings Terms and Sources and the definition of every term were drafted by the architect and approved by Austin word for word. They live in `memo/render_page_words.py` and are pinned against a copy typed again from the brief, so the test also checks the transcription. Each Nasdaq status quote is held equal to the text after its label in `tools/directory_codes.STATUS_MEANINGS`, and the note for a selection with no rule in force is a copy of `tools/field_text.NOT_IN_FORCE_NOTE`, held equal to it, because the memo may not import that module.
- The terms. A term is a value of one of nine fields of the engine's vocabulary, 30 words over 34 field and word pairs, each with its own definition, and each definition begins with its context ("Of a finding:", "Of a verdict:", "Why a rule was not measured:"). The Terms section lists each word that appears in the memo once, in order of first appearance, with one definition for each field it is a value of in this memo, also in order of first appearance. That happens in 5 of run 2's 9 drafts: clear as a finding's state and as the reported financials signal (aacg, agmh, luckin), and breached likewise (aixi.three, trans_american.three). Over the 42 recorded sets, the most frequent terms are unknown (661 facts), input_required (507) and false (88); 13 of the 34 pairs never occur (at_risk under all three fields, meets, global_select, no_xbrl_facts, period_unavailable, and the six statuses other than normal and deficient). A test holds the definitions total over the members of every vocabulary.
- The dek. Its first sentence is the tickers (joined with a comma and the last with "and") and the listing question, or the question alone with its first letter capitalized when the company has no ticker; the second names the date evaluated; a third appears when an assessed tier has no rule in force (the approved note when none has, a sentence naming the tiers when only some have); a fourth appears when the exchange's directory could not be read. The 42 recorded sets have 14 cases: continued listing with the tier named (capital or global_select, 1 or 3 tickers), from the directory (capital twice, global once), no tier because no ticker matched the directory (3 sets) or because every match is an exchange-traded product (Credit Suisse, GLDI, SLVO and USOI); initial listing by traditional IPO against all three tiers or on the named Global tier; and initial listing by SPAC IPO, where no rule is in force for any tier (7 sets). The directory was available in all 42, and none has no ticker, several tiers, a category not recognized or another pathway; those cases are tested on headers and contexts built by hand. Two facts from the tier selection (`tools/tier_selection.py`) decide what the dek can meet. An initial listing is decided before the directory is read, as named when a tier is asked for and against all three otherwise, so an initial listing with any other tier choice is refused with `RenderRefused`. When the directory could not be read, the selection goes on only for a named tier, an initial listing and a company with no ticker, and raises for a continued listing that would take its tier from the directory; the fourth sentence is tested on those three, built by hand.
- The footer. Three lines, from the provenance and the record: who wrote the narrative, at what effort, on the first attempt or after one retry, and the start of the prompt's hash; that the narrative was checked against the checker's rules with no findings (the count is read from the checker's rule list, never typed, 24 today), the model's cost exactly as the meter recorded it, the tokens and the price table's version; and the record's format, when it was made (date, time and UTC) and the start of the fact set's digest. The two hashes show their first 16 hexadecimal characters after the word "beginning": a 64-character unbroken string overflows a phone screen under the approved style, which stays unchanged, and the record keeps the full values.
- Two changes to D71's text layer, both for the page. A rule approved in an SEC release has two links, and the page links its label to the rule and the words "Approved in" and the release to the release; so a source now carries the release apart from its label, and the approval sentence is written by one function that the plain-text projection and the page share. And the marks are now built from runs of numbers that the page can link one by one. The plain-text projections of run 2's nine drafts keep their pinned digests, which proves their bytes did not change.
- Safety. Every text and every attribute value is escaped with quotes. The only links are the page's own fragments and the links D71 built and checked, each checked again by the page before it is written, with rel set to noopener noreferrer; the page builds none. There is no script, no style attribute, no form, no frame and nothing loaded from anywhere. The Content-Security-Policy is default-src 'none', the one style allowed by its SHA-256, base-uri 'none' and form-action 'none' (the last two are not covered by default-src). A test parses every page with html.parser and holds every tag and attribute to an allow-list that fails closed, every link to a fragment of the page or a checked link, and the policy's hash to one it recomputes from the style text with its own hashlib and base64 calls. Markup, quotes and an ampersand planted in the company's name, a ticker, a text, a value, an amount, a source label and the model id show only as inert text. The same planted in the recorded tool results (the company's name and the directory row's symbol and security name) and built into facts by the real builders also shows only as inert text, and the security name is never shown at all, because no label carries it. A form type with markup never reaches the page: the fact builders refuse it, because a form type becomes part of a fact id.
- Determinism. No clock, no randomness, no locale: the same record always gives the same bytes, ASCII only, LF line endings and one final newline, and a page that would hold anything else is refused. The nine pages of run 2 are pinned by digest and size: ge.one 19,673 bytes, credit_suisse.one 20,859, agmh.one 21,463, aacg.one 21,670, luckin.one 22,986, trans_american.one 23,343, aixi.one 25,059, trans_american.three 42,069 and aixi.three 45,776. No size ceiling is pinned: the digests pin every byte and the limits of D66 bound a narrative.
- Evidence: the suite of 5,241 passing tests and the 3 known failures, and 111 scripted mutations, each restored byte for byte and each caught by a named test: escaping removed from each slot or done without quotes; a link built, swapped, unchecked, without rel, or pointing a range to its last source or a source off by one; the release link dropped or its words changed; the policy's hash over other bytes, each directive dropped or loosened; a character, a line or the final newline of the style; a script or a style attribute emitted; the reading label with its colon or without the space after it; a term shown plain; every value or no count set as a figure; the marks dropped or spaced; the exchange note moved or dropped; a section without claims shown; a heading changed; the terms reordered, collapsed to one field, shown as members, or a definition missing or reworded; each footer figure taken from the wrong field, rounded, typed or unformatted; each dek sentence dropped, inverted or reworded; the header's tiers, directory flag or name taken wrongly; the ASCII and line-ending checks; the composition's stamp; D71's two changes; and a forbidden import in a page module. The recordings of both runs are byte for byte unchanged.
- Options considered: a page that finds the approval sentence inside the label (rejected: it parses free text); a size ceiling (rejected: the digests pin every byte); an error type of its own (rejected: the existing refusal of the renderer says what is wrong); definitions keyed by the word alone (rejected: clear and breached mean different things for a finding and for the reported financials signal); full hashes in the footer (rejected: they overflow a phone screen).
- Why: the page is the one form of the memo most readers will see, so everything on it is either the approved words, a value the program put in, or a link the program checked, and nothing in the data can turn into markup, a script or a request.
- Revisit if: a vocabulary gains a member (the definitions are tested total), a fact gains a field the page shows, the selection gains a tier choice or a pathway, Austin changes the look or the words, or the page is served from somewhere other than a file (then the policy belongs in a header).

### D72: The memo record and its validator (second of three commits that turn a checked narrative into a memo)
- Date: 2026-11-22
- Decision: A memo is kept as a record, `MemoRecord` in `workflow/record.py`, and every record is checked by a validator, `check_record` in `workflow/verify.py`, both when it is built and when it is loaded from JSON. The record holds what a stored memo needs to be shown again and to be checked again, and no word that a prompt or a response says: the format string `memo-record-v1`, `created_at`, the run's context, the fact set and its digest, the narrative, who wrote it (model, effort, the output limit, the cache setting and the SHA-256 of the system text that was actually sent), the number of attempts, the findings of the first attempt (rules and places, never text), the calls the meter recorded (figures, never text), their totals with the version of the price table, and the ids of the statements the memo carries. The complete field set of the record and of the writer's identity is pinned as an exact literal, so a field cannot be added or renamed without a test failing.
- `created_at` is in the first version, required, timezone-aware UTC and whole seconds, and the caller supplies it: nothing in `memo/` or `workflow/` reads a clock. It is checked for its shape only and compared with nothing, because a run can cross midnight UTC while the date of the rules (D65) stays one day. Adding a field later would have moved the format, so it is here from the start.
- The validator, check by check and the misuse that reaches each. It refuses with `RecordInvalid(check, detail)`, where the check is a fixed word and the detail one of seventeen fixed sentences, or, for a narrative, the names of the checker's rules and nothing else; no value, id of the draft or word of the narrative is ever in a message, and a test holds every detail to that literal set. (1) The format must be the current one: a record of another version. (2) `created_at` must be UTC and in whole seconds: a naive time, a time in another zone, a fractional second. (3) The prompt hash must be 64 lowercase hexadecimal characters. (4) The statements must equal the ids of `memo.statements.STATEMENT_IDS` in order: an unknown, missing or reordered one. (5) The digest is recomputed from the fact set: a hand-edited fact set or digest. (6) The writer's view is rebuilt from the fact set and the checker is run on the narrative, and any finding refuses the record: a stored narrative edited after it was checked, or a checker that has since got stricter. (7) The context must agree with the facts: the directory file's hash and stamp equal those of every directory citation, a requested tier is a tier that has a verdict, the tiers without rules have none, and an exchange-status fact needs a directory that was available; the plausible bug here is a fact set paired with the context of another run. (8) The purposes of the calls are an allow-list that fails closed: the writer's call, then its retry, and nothing else, so the reading calls of a later phase join it by a decision; the attempts equal the number of calls (one or two), the first attempt's findings are present exactly when there was a retry, every call completed, and each call's requested and served model equal the writer's. (9) The totals equal the sums over the calls: attempts, tokens, exact Decimal dollars and failed calls (none, since a failed call has no draft). Checks 8 and 9 can only fail on a load or an edit, because the builder takes the calls and the totals from the meter; they make a bug unreachable and a hand edit refused.
- The prompt hash is checked for its form and never compared with today's prompt. A record made under D68's prompt must still load after the prompt changes, so the hash says which text was sent and is not a claim that it is the current one. A test builds a two-attempt record from run 1 under D68's prompt (hash `ae377c1c...a3b8`, not D69's `e31e758a...eb07`) and shows it validates against today's checker and today's view.
- The two format pins. `tests/test_workflow_record_format.py` couples `memo-record-v1` to a digest over what decides the checker's verdict (the 24 rule names and their fixed messages in order, the digest of D69's frozen lists, the section tables and the rule for counts, the placeholder pattern and fields, the limits of a narrative, and the writer's view of the golden Xiao-I set with the facts it marks primary, whose view digest is D69's `5cf08588...71d3`) and over the field sets of the record and of the models it holds. If any of it changes, the test fails with "the checker changed: a decision must bump the record format first": a stored record was valid under the checker it was made with, and a change to the checker needs a new format and a decision, not a quiet edit. The view was added in review: the architect's ruling had left it out, and the architect showed that a change to what the view shows passed the first version of the pin and fails this one. `tests/test_memo_facts_format.py` keeps `memo-facts-v1`, which nothing stored before, and couples it to the golden Xiao-I set (digest `bf4f354b...498f`, 57,364 bytes) and to the pinned field sets of every stored model, failing with "a stored fact's shape changed: a decision must bump the format first". Each part of each pin is tested to change the digest when it changes.
- `Provenance`, in `memo/render_model.py` and pure: the model id, the effort, the prompt hash, the cost as the exact decimal text of the meter's dollars, the attempts, the tokens and the version of the price table. `workflow/record.py` builds it from a record, so the page of D73 needs nothing of the model interface and the footer cannot show a figure that the record does not hold.
- The canaries. With `LISTINGLENS_ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` and `SEC_EDGAR_CONTACT` set to canary strings, a record built and dumped to JSON holds neither, and neither does its text form or its provenance. Four mutations that let the key or the contact reach the record, the writer's model or the provenance are each caught by that one test.
- The figures the architect derived, reproduced from the recordings. agmh.one of run 2 is one call, purpose `memo.writer`, model `claude-sonnet-5-5`, 12,141 tokens (6,029 input, 2,795 cache read, 3,317 output), 0.0455075 dollars, prompt hash `e31e758a...eb07`, fact set digest beginning `7e6a5a6d3e0eac70`, no first findings. aacg.one, the call that wrote the cache, is 11,946 tokens (5,940 input, 2,795 five-minute cache write, 3,211 output) and 0.0509775 dollars. The nine run-2 records' totals sum to 0.5170635 dollars and 137,995 tokens, the figures of D70.
- The two-attempt case is agmh.one of run 1, under D68's prompt: the first attempt had two findings from today's checker (`quantity_word` in not_measured claim 3 and `ref_not_for_section` in verdict claim 2), the retry has none. By hand at the price table's rates per million tokens for Claude Sonnet 5.5 (input 2.00, cache read 0.10, output 10.00): the first call is 5,928 input, 2,609 cache read and 3,288 output, which is 11,856 + 260.9 + 32,880 = 44,996.9, so 0.0449969 dollars; the retry is 9,307 input, 2,609 cache read and 3,615 output, which is 18,614 + 260.9 + 36,150 = 55,024.9, so 0.0550249 dollars; together 0.1000218 dollars and 8,537 + 3,288 + 11,916 + 3,615 = 27,356 tokens, which are the record's totals. Seven of the eight run-1 pairs have a retry that passes today's checker; the retry of aacg.one still has two findings, so it could not be a record. agmh.one is the draft used because its first attempt also has findings under today's checker, which a two-attempt record requires.
- Evidence: the suite of 5,137 passing tests and the 3 known failures, and 113 scripted mutations, each restored byte for byte and each caught by a named test: every validator check removed, inverted or loosened by one (a time in another zone, a tenth of a second, a short, long or upper-case hash, a reordered, missing or unknown statement, a digest forged, emptied or changed in its last character, the checker call skipped, the purposes turned into a deny-list, accepted in any order or by prefix, no calls, each of the models, the totals' attempts, tokens, failed calls and dollars unchecked or loosened, each group of checks skipped), every detail that echoed a value, the validator not running on build or on load, the format string changed, the prompt hash compared with today's prompt, each field of the record, the writer and the provenance, and each input of both pins (a rule message, the order of the rules, a limit, a section table, the count rule, a placeholder field and pattern, a frozen list's window, a stored field). The recordings of both runs are byte for byte unchanged.
- Options considered: the record in `memo/` with its own copies of the call figures (rejected: it duplicates the strict models of `CallRecord` and needs a converter in `workflow/` anyway, and the record is built from the writer's result); a package of its own (rejected: a third boundary guard and no gain); a stored identity of the checker in the record instead of the format pin (rejected: it would be a second source of truth for what the pin already says, and a stored identity cannot fail a test when someone edits the checker); bumping `memo-facts-v1` now (rejected: nothing stored before, so a bump protects nothing and moves every digest).
- Why: a memo that is published must be able to prove, years later, which facts it came from, which checker passed it, how many tries it took and what it cost, and a record that cannot be edited without a test or a validator saying so is the only form of that proof that does not depend on trusting whoever stored it.
- Revisit if: the memo command stores records (where they are saved, how they are read back, and whether a record from an older checker is shown with a note), the reading calls of a later phase join the purposes, or the checker or the shape of a fact changes, which needs a new format string and a decision first.

### D71: The text layer of the renderer (first of three commits that turn a checked narrative into a memo)
- Date: 2026-11-22
- Decision: The renderer and the memo record land in three commits, not one. The plan came to about 1,240 lines of source and 1,800 of tests, D64 had refused one commit of about 1,400 source lines, and the two halves do not import each other. D71 is the text layer, which is this record: value formatting, term marking, placeholder filling, source numbering and links, the statements, a rendered document model and its plain-text projection. D72 is the record of a run (the record model and its validator in `workflow/`, with a plain provenance in `memo/`), and D73 is the HTML page. D71's rendered model has no provenance.
- The rendering rules, approved by Austin after he read a preview page built from run 2's agmh.one draft. (1) A word of the engine's own vocabulary is shown as the exact member with each underscore a space and is marked as a term, because the writer uses those words as labels ("State: {x}", "The verdict is {x}"): data_status.status, gap.reason, nasdaq_status.status and tier, finding.state, not_measured.primary, condition_status.value, reported_financials.signal and verdict.verdict. There are no word tables for them. (2) Word tables exist only for plain nouns and units, each total over its vocabulary and pinned: the nine concepts of a gap (stockholders' equity, net income, pretax income, total assets, revenue, cash, shares outstanding, public float, income from continuing operations) and the four units of a finding (USD, USD per share, shares, count). (3) Identity texts print as they are (the page escapes them): the company text, the form of a filing and of a count, the exchange's symbol, market category and financial status, the section of a rule, a standard's name and a figure's unit. (4) Amounts and counts are exact: the fixed-point text with a comma after each group of three digits of the integer part, a leading hyphen-minus for a negative, the fraction kept exactly as written, never rounded or scaled. (5) Dates are "November 4, 2026", from a table of month names, never from the locale; the record keeps ISO dates. (6) A figure's period is the bare date for an instant and "January 1, 2025 to December 31, 2025" for a duration. (7) The renderer never edits the writer's words: no grammar fix of any kind, and the prose between the values is copied byte for byte (a test compares it with the text split at the placeholders, for all nine drafts). The 30 rules, one for each field a placeholder may name, are a table whose keys a test holds equal to the placeholder allowlist, and every rule is run over every value of every fact of all 42 recorded sets (4,193 facts, 9,502 pairs, of which 1,440 are null: the standard name of a branch condition, the form of a count that is not a count of a form, and a filing's report date, the only three fields that are ever null, and the checker already refuses a placeholder on them).
- The renderer checks first. Its one entry point, `render_memo`, builds the writer's view from the fact set and runs the checker before it fills anything, and refuses a narrative with any finding with `RenderRefused` naming the rules and nothing of the narrative, so no path renders an unchecked narrative, with a record or without one. A test renders narratives with one finding each (a digit, advice, a field the kind does not allow, an id that is not a fact) and a mutation that removes the call is caught by it. `RenderRefused` is otherwise kept for a field with no rule, a null value, a member with no word and a value of the wrong kind, which the totality tests reach by calling the rules directly; a malformed date cannot arise (the values are typed dates).
- The statements. Three, each under an id that ends in its version, in `memo/statements.py`, which holds the ids and the texts because the plain-text projection is a form of the memo and must carry the disclosure wherever the memo is shown: `scope_note_v1` is the scope note every tool result carries (imported), `exchange_note_v1` is the text of the directory's status note (copied, because the memo may not import that module, with a test that the copy equals the constant) and `ai_disclosure_v1` was drafted by the architect and approved word for word by Austin: "A program took every figure from the company's SEC filings, measured each against the Nasdaq rules, read the exchange's own directory and cited every fact. An AI model wrote the sentences below from those facts alone, without seeing any filing text. The program checked every sentence, put in every value and attached every source. Passages marked Reading are the model's interpretation, not facts." A changed text gets a new id by decision. All three are pinned against an independent copy.
- The sources. A citation gets one number, in order of first use: sections in reading order, claims in order, within a claim the refs and then the basis as listed, within a fact its citation ids as the fact lists them. A claim carries the sorted numbers of all its citations; a run of two or more consecutive numbers is a range ("3-4", "2-22") and the others are joined by commas with no space ("3,7-9"). Over the nine drafts of run 2 the claims and distinct sources are aacg 21 and 40, agmh 21 and 39, aixi one tier 25 and 45, credit_suisse 20 and 40, ge 18 and 39, luckin 22 and 43, trans_american one tier 20 and 45, aixi three tiers 30 and 83, trans_american three tiers 24 and 83, counted by a plain walk of each draft in the test. The words of each source label were written and approved word for word by the architect, as Austin saw them on the preview, and are pinned with their values filled by the approved rules (dates as "November 4, 2026", everything else verbatim): a filing row, each field of the submissions record (CIK, company name, entity type, tickers, exchanges, filing list), the coverage of the company facts, the fiscal-year anchor, an XBRL fact (an instant and a duration), a rule (with and without its release) and a directory row. A rule's quoted text is Nasdaq's own words and stays verbatim: 5 of the 62 distinct quotes in the recorded sets contain a semicolon, none a double quote and none a newline.
- The links. Every link is built by code from the fields of the citation and passes an allow-list that fails closed: https only, the host exactly one of www.sec.gov, data.sec.gov, listingcenter.nasdaq.com and www.nasdaqtrader.com, and no userinfo, port, query, fragment, space or character outside printable ASCII; an unreadable address, a CIK that is not ten digits and an accession number not in the SEC's form are refused too, with `LinkRefused`, which names the kind of citation and never the address. The tests refuse the host as a suffix, a substring and a prefix of an allowed one (www.sec.gov.example.com, evilsec.gov, evilwww.sec.gov, sec.gov) and the scripted mutations turn the host check into each of those matches. A filing row links to its own index address, a submissions field to the submissions JSON, the coverage to the company-facts JSON, the fiscal-year anchor and an XBRL fact to the filing's index built from the CIK without leading zeros and the accession without dashes (the 840 filing rows in the recorded sets carry exactly that address), a rule to its address and its release to the release's, a directory row to its address. The hosts that occur in the stored citations of all 42 sets (2,107 citations: 667 rule, 1,164 record, 240 XBRL, 36 directory) are listingcenter.nasdaq.com (667), www.sec.gov (667 release addresses and 840 filing rows) and www.nasdaqtrader.com (36); data.sec.gov occurs in none and enters only through the built links, so all four hosts of the list are used. A new rule host in a ruleset is refused at render time, loudly.
- The plain-text projection, approved in its form by the architect: the scope note, a blank line, the AI disclosure, a blank line; then each section that has claims, its heading on a line of its own (Reported financials, Findings, Standards, Not measured, Exchange status, Filings, Verdict), the exchange note as the first paragraph under Exchange status (followed by a blank line), one line for each claim, an inference beginning "Reading: ", each claim ending in a space and its marks in square brackets, and a blank line after the section; then the line Sources and one line for each source, "{n}. {label}", each followed by its links one to a line indented four spaces. Terms are plain words, there is no wrapping, the endings are LF, the text ends in one newline and is ASCII only; there is no header and no footer (the page adds them). The nine projections of run 2 are pinned by digest, and the sentences of agmh.one and trans_american.one are checked by hand against values read with plain json from the raw fixture files (44,228,907 and 39,172,562 and 325,338 for agmh; 1,106,686, 509 and -3,758,603 at September 30, 2025 for trans_american; the filing dates from the raw submissions).
- Three inputs for the Phase 5 evaluation, recorded and not fixed by the renderer. Of the 5 places in run 2 where "a" or "an" stands directly before a placeholder, one disagrees with the value (agmh.one renders "A NT 20-F"). Of the 18 count placeholders whose value is one and that are followed by a noun, none sits beside a plural (D70). aixi.one renders "large reported loss of -101,221,046", a negative amount after a word that already says it. The prompt owes one line for these at its next change, telling the writer not to place an article, a plural or a word that repeats a sign directly beside a placeholder whose value decides it; the renderer does not fix any of them, because it never edits the writer's words.
- Evidence: the suite of 5,042 passing tests and the 3 known failures, and 132 scripted mutations (the grouping, sign, fraction, rounding, month, day and period of the formatters; each rule, each word of both tables and each class; the removal of the checker's call; the prose edit, the order and the missing last piece of the filling; each host removed, a host added, the suffix, substring and prefix matches, http, a query, a fragment, any character, the CIK's zeros, the accession's dashes and each link not checked; the marks' sorting, merging, end and separator; the first-use order of refs and basis, a fact's citation order, renumbering and unsorted claim numbers; each source label word; the headings and the reading label; the structure of the projection; the statements; and the guard against a connection, a locale and strftime), each restored byte for byte and each caught by a named test. The 26 recordings of run 1 and run 2 and their two manifests were never touched (the sha256 of each of the 28 files was taken before and after).
- Options considered: rounding or scaling amounts (rejected: the writer's figures must be exact); word tables for the engine's vocabulary (rejected: the writer uses the members as labels); fixing an article or a plural in the renderer (rejected: it never edits the writer's words); the renderer trusting the record's validator to have checked the narrative (rejected: a narrative could be rendered without a record); `strftime` for dates (rejected: the locale would change the page with the machine); a prefix or suffix host match (rejected, and tested); one commit for the text layer, the record and the page (rejected as too large).
- Why: the memo is the one place the writer's sentences meet the facts, so every value is filled by a rule that can be read in one line, every source is numbered and linked by code that cannot send a reader off the four hosts, and nothing is rendered that the checker has not passed.
- Revisit if: a ruleset adds a host, a fact gains a field a placeholder may name (the keys of the table must equal the allowlist), a vocabulary gains a member (the word tables are tested total), the preview of D73 shows a sentence that reads wrongly, or Austin changes a label or a statement (a new id).

### D70: Run 2: every draft passed on the first attempt (the recordings of the second live run)
- Date: 2026-11-21
- Decision: Austin ran `scripts/draft_memos.py` a second time, with D69's prompt and fact set, and its 9 recordings and manifest are committed unchanged in `tests/fixtures/llm/live_writer/run-2/` (the sha256 of each of the 10 files was taken before staging and compared with the manifest's, and the staged blobs equal the files). All 9 drafts passed on the first attempt, with no retry and no failure. `RUN` in the script is now `run-3`, which the naming test of D69 requires once `run-2` exists. The checks every committed run shares (the folder, the manifest byte for byte, the one system prompt by its hash, the planned requests, the replay through the fake client and the meter) moved into `tests/live_run_support.py`; run 1's test keeps every check it had (6 tests and 29 assertions before and after, each now made by the shared function with run 1's pinned values, and its four D69 mutations are caught by the refactored file), and run 2's test is that support plus what is particular to run 2. A later run adds one small file, not a copy.
- Run 2 beside run 1. First attempts that passed: 9 of 9 against 1 of 9 (GE). Retries: none against 8. Failures: none in either. Cost: 0.5170635 dollars against 1.1081229. Tokens: 137,995 against 299,754. The nine first calls alone cost 0.5171 against 0.5345: output tokens 35,270 against 37,228 (1,958 fewer, about 0.020 dollars) and prompt tokens 102,725 against 100,305 (2,420 more, about 0.005). D69 had expected about 0.54 for the nine first calls and 0.61 to 0.76 in all, with one to three retries. The largest output was 8,010 of the 12,000 allowed tokens (Xiao-I with three tiers, which also holds the only thinking tokens, 1,313: thinking was zero on 8 of the 9 calls). The cached prefix was 2,795 tokens (2,609 in run 1), written by the first call and read by the other eight, which saved about 0.0425 dollars at the difference between the input and the cache-read price, less a write premium of about 0.0014. The prompt was 2.13 to 2.15 characters a token (2.11 to 2.16 in run 1). Every request carries D69's prompt word for word, pinned by the sha256 `e31e758a1af3476ea457d9a6b4dfbb3a0ba93504fe4d7eabacc3da3f1625eb07` (6,063 characters), taken from `git show d28930a` by reading its paragraphs with `ast`, not from the code, and the nine fingerprints equal the nine requests the scenario builds at d28930a.
- The drafts. 201 claims (18 to 30 in a draft: aacg 21, agmh 21, aixi one tier 25, credit_suisse 20, ge 18, luckin 22, trans_american one tier 20, aixi three tiers 30, trans_american three tiers 24), of which 16 are inferences, and 652 placeholders. Today's checker finds nothing on any of the nine, and every placeholder is on a field its fact holds.
- What each fix of D69 did. Form counts: 54 placeholders name a form through `form_type` (3, 5, 8, 6, 5, 5, 7, 7 and 8 in the order above), and no form is written in prose, so there is no digit finding on a form. The gaps in two sections: the count of the gaps is referenced in a not_measured claim in all nine drafts, with no section finding. The prompt: no claim's prose restates that the memo is a risk signal, is not investment advice or was written by an AI (the three phrases, found by search, occur nowhere), no test is called "two-of-three", and no inference lists a fact of another section in refs. The new rule, `field_not_carried`, never fired: not one placeholder is on a field without a value.
- For the evaluation of Phase 5. The 16 inferences use judgment words that no checker rule measures: "fairly narrow" (AGMH: income from continuing operations of 577,826 dollars against a threshold of 500,000, so it clears by about 16 percent), "wide" (Xiao-I with three tiers: reported equity of minus 100,554,759 dollars, which is 104,554,759 below even the lowest equity threshold of 4,000,000), "very small" (Trans American: reported cash of 509 dollars) and "financial strain" (Xiao-I with one tier: a reported net loss of 101,221,046 dollars against total assets of 15,901,131); the architect checked these four against their figures and they were faithful. "Pressure on listing standing" (Xiao-I with one tier, the verdict) is a reading of the breaches and the exchange's flag. One reading overreaches: in the findings of Xiao-I with three tiers, negative reported equity "drives the breaches in every tier", worded as a reading and not a verified cause, when some of the breaches in those tiers are assets and revenue rules. Nothing in the checker measures a judgment word or an inferred cause, so this is an input to the evaluation case that tries to draw a view out of the writer, not an entry for a list (the lists are frozen by D69).
- For the renderer. A count is placed in a sentence by the writer, which reads the value in the view, and the renderer will put the value in without changing the noun beside it, and nothing in the checker agrees a noun with a count. That is a real risk and not one run 2 realized: of the 132 count placeholders followed by a noun, 18 have the value one, and in all 18 the writer chose a singular or neutral word ("fiscal year", "figure was extracted", "of that form", "is not"); the nearest plurals are labels such as "Standards true" followed by a count. A count of one beside a plural noun is therefore a thing the renderer, or a rule of the checker, must handle, not a thing seen.
- The bill. The Claude Console shows 1.11 dollars for the workspace, which matches the meter's own figures: run 1's 1.1081229 plus the format proof's 0.00139052 (D63), about 1.1095. The comparison D62 and D63 owed against the bill is therefore closed for the calls that reading covers, This checks the meter's total against the Console's to the cent, not each rate on its own.
- Evidence: the suite of 4,886 passing tests and the 3 known failures, and 55 scripted mutations (the `RUN` constant set to the folder of run 2 and to run 4 and the output folder not named by it; the pinned prompt hash, the manifest hash and a pinned figure of run 2's test, each of the figures in turn; a draft's name; the shared support's folder, manifest, prompt, request and replay checks, each removed and each caught by run 1's and by run 2's tests; and changes to the section table, the placeholder allowlist and the view's carried keys that make today's checker find something on the nine drafts), each restored byte for byte and each caught by a named test, with run 1's four mutations of D69 run again against the refactored test. The recordings were never mutated.
- Options considered: leaving run 1's test as it was and copying its checks for run 2 (rejected: a third run would copy them again); pinning run 2 to the requests today's scenario builds (rejected: the test would fail the day the prompt or the view changes, which is not a fault of run 2); a rule of the checker for a count beside a noun (not made: no instance has been seen).
- Why: the second run is the measurement that D69 asked for, and a committed run with a test that pins its prompt, its manifest, its figures and what today's checker says of it is evidence that a later change can be held to.
- Revisit if: a later change to the prompt, the view or the checker makes a recorded draft fail a check (a new run and a new record), a run needs a retry for a cause the checker found, a renderer places a count beside a noun of the wrong number, or the Console's figure after a later run differs from the meter's.

### D69: What run 1 showed: form counts, the gaps in two sections, the writer's second prompt, the classification and the frozen lists (fourth of four narrative commits)
- Date: 2026-11-21
- Decision: Austin ran `scripts/draft_memos.py` once. It wrote 17 recordings and a manifest, 9 first attempts and 8 retries; 8 drafts passed (GE's on the first attempt and seven on the retry), the draft for AACG was rejected after its retry and none failed. It cost 1.1081229 dollars (the nine first calls 0.5345377, the eight retries 0.5735852; 0.031 to 0.054 for a one-tier first call and 0.10 to 0.12 for a three-tier one) and 299,754 tokens, and the billed prompt tokens equalled the recorded usage on all 17 calls. The prompt was 2.11 to 2.16 characters a token, not the 2.7 that D66 and D68 assumed; the bound the client reported was about 12 percent above the billed prompt (1.1 to 2.2 thousand tokens); thinking tokens were zero on nine calls and 322 to 1,251 on the other eight; the worst case held for a first call was 0.157 to 0.206 dollars and for a retry up to 0.236 (D68 had said 0.15 to 0.19; its sum of about 1.45 for the nine first calls was close to the real 1.43 because the two errors offset). The recordings were moved, unchanged, into `tests/fixtures/llm/live_writer/run-1/` (the sha256 of each of the 18 files was taken before and after the move and the two lists are identical, and each file matches the manifest), and a test pins that they load, match the manifest byte for byte, carry the prompt of commit 3 (the sha256 `ae377c1cb2c52180ce8ea436855faba0335c588c9a57e72d5a588af27e9da3b8` of its text, taken from `git show e3857b8` by reading its paragraphs with `ast`, not from the code), and replay through the fake client and the meter with the figures above. The drafting script now writes to a folder named by one constant, `RUN`, set to `run-2`; a test requires the existing run folders to be `run-1` to `run-K` without a gap and `RUN` to be the next name, so the commit that adds a run's recordings names the next run, and the script still refuses a folder that holds any entry. The shared fixture check treats a folder whose entries are all `run-N` folders as a container and each run folder as a scenario.
- The classification. The checker found 17 things on the 17 recorded drafts, 15 on first attempts and 2 on AACG's retry, and today's checker, over the recorded drafts and the nine views built today, gives exactly the 15 that were right and not the 2 that were wrong. The table is frozen in `tests/memo_classification.py`, one row for each finding (file, rule, section, claim, true catch or false positive, cause), and a test recomputes it, confirms that the fact ids and kinds of the nine views are the ones the writer was shown, and shows the two false positives gone. Seven digit findings were true catches (the writer wrote form names beside counts: Schedule 13D, form 6-K, 424B5, F-6 POS) and their cause is a missing field, which is why AACG was rejected: a count had no field for its form, and the retry repeated the error. Refinement: a legal but awkward route existed through the form_type of a filing fact of the same form, which every form count has in the same view, and the writer never took it. Five quantity findings were true catches: three claims called a test "two-of-three" (a prompt cause), "Two concepts were not found" (a table cause, because the placeholder it needed, the count of the gaps, was not allowed in not_measured) and "For the first two" (the writer). Three findings were verdict inferences that listed the exchange's flag, a finding or a findings count in refs (a prompt cause, true catches). Two findings were false positives, a claim in not_measured that listed the count of the gaps (a table cause). By cause: missing field 7, prompt 6, table 3, writer 1. The list entries that matched were `two` (five times) and `three` (three times, inside "two-of-three"), all in true catches. Refinement: neither the recommendation list nor the prediction list fired once on any of the 17 drafts, and the passing drafts hold no advice, rating or prediction, so the evidence for those two lists is silence, not measurement.
- Form counts. A count of one form of filing now carries the form's exact name in `form_type` (the SEC's own spelling: 6-K, SCHEDULE 13G/A). The value is set exactly on the counts whose name begins `filings.form.` and on no other count, its slug must equal the name's, and every filing the count counts must have exactly that form (the population check compares slugs, so two spellings of one slug would have passed it); the fact set refuses a violation loudly. The key is left out of every serialized form when it is absent (python and json dumps, the JSON text and the canonical JSON of a fact set), so every other count is byte for byte what it was, and a fact set with form counts round-trips through dump and validate unchanged. The placeholder allowlist for a count is now `count` and `form_type`. The writer's view carries, for each fact it shows, the keys of the shown row that hold a value (`carried`), and a test shows that over every row of all 42 recorded sets `carried` equals the non-null keys of that row's JSON as it appears in the view text, so it and what the writer sees cannot disagree. A new checker rule, `field_not_carried`, fires on a placeholder whose field is in the allowlist of its kind but is absent or null in the fact: its fixed message is "A placeholder names a field that this fact has no value for.", it sits directly after `field_not_allowed` in the order of the rules (there are now 24), and a placeholder fires one of the two, never both. Only two allowlisted fields are ever null in the recorded sets: `report_date` of a filing (210 of 840 rows) and `standard_name` of a condition (97 of 225, the branch and requirement conditions); before this rule the checker never saw a value, so a placeholder on a null field passed, and none of the 17 drafts has one (0 of 1,324 placeholders). The fact set holds the same facts and citations as before (96 facts and 50 citations for Xiao-I, 83 facts in its view): a form is a value, not a fact.
- Pinned values that changed, each re-derived by hand: a form count row is longer by 15 plus the length of its form, so Xiao-I's seven forms (20-F, 20-F/A, 424B5, 6-K, F-6 POS, SCHEDULE 13G, SCHEDULE 13G/A) add 19 + 21 + 20 + 18 + 22 + 27 + 29 = 156 bytes. Its view goes from 14,390 to 14,546 bytes and its SHA-256 from `3b95539b...988e03` to `5cf08588ee5b8860f5279fc0f200c9ddcb0a1a045fd4f497cbc48bb2746a71d3`, and a test takes the form keys out of the new view by hand and gets D66's digest again. The view sizes over the seven recorded companies go from 12,016 to 15,602 bytes to 12,142 to 15,774 for one tier and from 24,738 to 35,782 to 24,862 to 35,970 for three tiers. The golden fact set of D65 (the recorded Xiao-I set, which holds the seven form counts) goes from 57,208 to 57,364 bytes and its digest from `d0e08fa1...3947ceb` to `bf4f354bacbc05d1fab4db95c43b96f209a56df38fd02c49ad835a970730498f`; with the form keys taken out it hashes to the old value. The digest of the hand-built set of D64 does not move (it holds no form count). The format string of a fact set, `memo-facts-v1`, is not changed: a fact set is built and read inside one process and never stored, so a version protects nothing yet, and a bump would move every serialized digest. The string becomes meaningful once fact sets are stored, so the commit that adds the memo record revisits it. The request fingerprints of the nine drafts change (the prompt and the views), and nothing replays them.
- The gaps in two sections. `count.gaps` belongs to reported_financials and to not_measured, so the function that said which section a count belongs to (`section_of_count`) is now `sections_of_count` and returns a tuple, empty for a name no section takes. Its callers were the checker's section rule, the narrative test and the prompt test; no other. The prompt says so, and a test over the counts of all recorded sets requires the prompt's names to be the ones the sets hold.
- The prompt. The system prompt was replaced and the architect approved it word for word. Five paragraphs changed: the program, not the writer, states that the memo is a risk signal, is not investment advice and was written by an AI (the writer is told never to write any of it, because every draft restated it); an inference may rest on facts from any section, listing those in basis and keeping in refs only the facts of its own section it is about; a rule or a test is named by its rule_section placeholder and plain words and never by the counting words in its name, with "a test over more than one fiscal year is a multi-year test"; the count `form_type` and the rule that a form of filing is named only through a form_type placeholder, and that a field shown as null gets no placeholder; and `count.gaps` among the not_measured counts. It is 6,063 characters (the limit of its length test is now 7,000), has no digit but the numbers of its limits paragraph, no semicolon, long dash or non-ASCII character, and is pinned in the tests against an independent copy. Every consistency test of D68 holds, with the tables that moved updated. New tests pin the new sentences and that the old wording is gone.
- Frozen lists. The lists of D67 (52 quantity words, 6 fraction phrases, 38 recommendation phrases, 12 prediction markers, 5 terms, 2 followers and the windows of four and three words) were pinned as exact literals, one test file for each group, with a firing test for each entry. Freezing adds a pinned copy of all of them with the SHA-256 `fbba49252047137f566f93126c90dcb3bb2c5b736bdea3b8ae76385fe5102c46` of its canonical JSON, and a test that fails with "These lists are frozen by D69. A change needs a new decision record that supersedes D69 and a new classification of recorded drafts." The code of the lists does not change. D67's five known misses pass every list and stay as they are: "Delisting looks all but inevitable", "It won't keep its listing for long", "Expect a delisting", "a strong candidate to buy" and "It seems cheap relative to peers"; they are inputs to the Phase 5 evaluation case that tries to draw out a buy or sell view or a prediction, not further entries. Two false-positive candidates stay as they are, and fire: "should buy back shares" (on `should buy`) and "certain companies face delisting" (on the marker `certain` and the term `delisting`); neither occurred in run 1. A test holds the misses to passing and the candidates to firing, and requires this record to name all seven.
- The workflow guard now reads `from listinglens.core import config` as the import of `listinglens.core.config`: it expands every from-import to the modules and names it imports before it checks the banned names, which it did not, because it read only the module named after `from`.
- The second run. The budget (20 attempts, 450,000 tokens, 2.00 dollars) does not change, nor do the model, the effort, the output limit, the retry or the nine drafts. The prompt grows by about 640 characters and the views by 124 to 191 bytes, about 400 tokens a call, so the nine first calls should cost about 0.54 dollars; the three causes behind most of run 1's retries are fixed in the schema, the table and the prompt, so one to three retries are expected, at about 0.07 each, about 0.61 to 0.76 dollars in all and 0.68 in the middle; the worst case, every draft retried, is about 1.19 dollars, about 325,000 tokens and 18 attempts, and the worst case held for one call (up to 0.24) leaves room under the cap at the last call. Austin runs it after this commit, and its recordings go in a small follow-up commit, D70, as D63's did, together with the next value of `RUN`.
- Evidence: the suite of 4,872 passing tests and the 3 known failures, and 80 scripted mutations of this commit (and the 185 of D67 run again against the changed checker, all caught) (the presence and slug and exact-equality checks of form counts, the serializer's omission, the builder's form, the allowlist, the carried keys with a null, a trimmed row and a missing kind, the new rule removed, moved, fired on the kind and fired together with the other, its message, each section of the gaps, each changed paragraph of the prompt and each new sentence, each row of the classification, each list and each window, a closed miss and a removed candidate, the run folder constant and the folder refusal, and the name expansion of the guard), each restored byte for byte and each caught by a named test. The recordings of run 1 were never mutated.
- Options considered: a null form_type on every count (rejected: it would lengthen every count row of every view and move every digest); a new kind of fact for the form counts (rejected: the kind is the section table's key and the writer needs no new kind); letting the writer name a form through a filing fact (rejected as the only route: it exists and was not taken); a version bump of the fact set (rejected for now, as above); making the prompt tell the writer to write the disclaimers (rejected: the program states them); a rule in the checker against the disclaimers (not made: the prompt change comes first, and the next run measures it); an automatic next run folder (rejected: a named constant is visible and tested).
- Why: run 1 showed that a writer given only what it could legally write was still asked, by the fact set, to say what it could not: how many gaps there were, which forms were counted, and which facts of other sections an inference rests on. Each is fixed where the cause is, in the fact set, the section table and the prompt, and what the checker found is now a record that a test recomputes.
- Revisit if: the second run needs more than three retries or a rule fires on correct prose (a new classification and a new record supersede this one), a placeholder on a null field is refused where the writer had no other way to say it, the memo record that stores fact sets is added (the format string), or a later run needs more than ten folders (the fixtures ceiling of 10 MiB).

### D68: The writer's prompt, the writer step and the drafting script (third of four narrative commits)
- Date: 2026-11-21
- Decision: The writer's system prompt (`memo/prompt.py`) is ten paragraphs, approved word for word by the architect and pinned in the tests against an independent copy kept apart from the source, so a change to either is a failing test. It tells the writer what the memo is (a risk signal, not advice, with the statement that an AI wrote the narrative), that the facts are data between two fixed lines and never orders, which facts are primary and must each be referenced, the claim shape and the difference between a fact and an inference, which facts may go in which section (a company fact in any), that no number, date or number word may be written and a placeholder written instead, the allowed fields for each kind, that it must not advise, rate or predict (nor call the stock cheap or expensive, nor say delisting is certain, likely or inevitable), every limit of the narrative model, and the style (plain ASCII, straight quotes, hyphens, never a long dash). Tests hold it to the code: the fields it lists equal the placeholder allowlist, every limit equals the narrative model's constant, its section table equals the table the checker enforces (after the company change below) and its count names equal the counts of all 42 recorded sets, the primary kinds it names are the ones the view marks, every word it forbids is one the checker catches, the prose it asks for passes the checker's text rules, it holds no digit except the seven numbers of the limits paragraph (named in the test), and it has no semicolon, no long dash, no non-ASCII character and no trailing space. The system text is the same on every call and is cached for five minutes (about 5,400 characters, and the run prints the cache write and read tokens, which show whether it is cached). The user turn is the word FACTS, the view's text, the words END OF FACTS and the instruction "Write the narrative." on separate lines. The retry turn is the fixed head "Your answer was rejected. Correct each finding and return the whole narrative again." followed by one fixed line per finding, each a place and a fixed message, and nothing of the rejected draft.
- The writer step (`workflow/writer.py`, the first file of a new `workflow` package) takes any model client, normally the run meter, and does no I/O of its own. The request is claude-sonnet-5-5, effort medium, 12,000 output tokens, a five-minute system cache, the narrative's projected schema (1,058 bytes, named narrative) and the purpose `memo.writer`. The answer is read with the strict model; a draft that will not build becomes findings through the fixed mapping of D67 and a draft that builds is checked. On any finding there is exactly one retry, whose turns are the first user turn, the draft as the assistant's turn and the retry turn, under the purpose `memo.writer_retry` and with every other field of the first request. A second rejection raises `NarrativeRejected`, which holds the findings of the last attempt and whose message names the rules only. A refused, cut-off or context-full answer raises `WriterFailed` with one of those words as its reason, and so does a completed answer with no text (the reason empty): never a memo and never a retry, because a retry would send the same request again and could only spend the budget twice. A passing result says how many attempts it took and keeps the findings of the first attempt, so the rules can be measured. A reference that is not even shaped like an id empties the claim's list of references in the model, so it is reported as an unresolved reference and as no reference; both are true and both are fixed words. A guard test for the package says it may import the standard library, pydantic, `listinglens.core`, the model interface of `listinglens.llm` (never the provider adapter or the factory that builds it) and `listinglens.memo`, never the SDK, mcp, an HTTP library, the server, ingestion, extraction or the engines, reads no environment variable and no clock and prints nothing, and that nothing outside the package imports it. It also refuses an import of the bare listinglens.llm package, whose top level re-exports the factory, and bans listinglens.core.config, whose settings read the environment and hold the key.
- Two changes outside the new files, both asked for: a company fact may now be named in any section, because a writer that names the company in the signal section would otherwise be rejected for nothing (the section table of D66 and its test, and the prompt, say so), and one test of the memo guard changed. That guard required that nothing outside `memo/` mention the memo; the workflow package must, so the rule now reads nothing outside `memo/` and `workflow/`, matched by the parts of the path and never by a string prefix (a sibling such as `workflows/` or a module `workflow_tools.py` is still caught, shown by a self-test, and a mutation that loosens the match to a prefix is caught by it). The workflow guard has the mirror rule: nothing outside `workflow/` imports it.
- The drafting script (`scripts/draft_memos.py`) follows D62. Without the exact gate (`LISTINGLENS_ALLOW_LIVE=1`) it exits 1 before reading settings, building a client or writing a file, then settings, then the key (exit 5 if missing); an argument is exit 2. It builds the nine fact sets offline with a fixed clock from the recorded fixtures, with the builders the tests use (the real tool stack over a routed mock transport; nothing leaves the process, and the test helpers are imported only inside the function that builds them, so a refused run loads none of them): one tier for each of the seven companies (continued, the directory's tier for the three that have one, Global Select named for the other four) and all three tiers for two of them, Xiao-I and Trans American. A meter over a capture wrapper holds the run to 20 attempts, 450,000 tokens and 2.00 dollars. Every call that returned a response is written as a live recording under `tests/fixtures/llm/live_writer/`, retries and failures included, and loaded back as in D62 (a recording that will not load is removed and stops the run); a call that raised has no response and is not recorded. The run refuses to start, with exit 1 after the key check and before any call, if the recordings folder already holds a file, so the recordings of a stopped run are never mixed with those of a later one. The folder is deleted to run again. A typed model error, a budget stop or a broken promise stops the run with the calls made so far recorded and exit 1; a rejected or failed draft is an outcome, not a stop. It prints, per call, the fingerprint prefix, the stop, the usage with the thinking tokens, the cost, the worst case held, the bound, the billed prompt tokens and the margin; per draft, the rules that fired on the first attempt with their counts and, for the three list rules, which list entries matched and how often (from `memo/measure.py`, which holds fixed rule names, list entries and counts only), and the outcome; then the totals. It never prints a word of a draft, a fact or the prompt, and no key. Its two test seams are named `client_source` and `output_dir`, not the names D62's seams have, so that the guard of the live script stays as it is; a second guard says the same of this script (the seams are named nowhere else, they are not reachable from the command line, it imports no provider or HTTP code and no sibling script, and the gate is named by no workflow and by no test other than the live script's own). Because these tests set the gate through the script's constant and never spell it, the guard also refuses any test function that starts a process and refers to that constant.
- Cost. The worst case the meter holds for a first call is about 0.15 dollars for one tier and 0.19 for three (bound taken as the request's characters at 2.7 per token plus a tenth plus 256 tokens, the figures of D66; the nine first calls sum to about 1.45 against the cap of 2.00), so the pre-call check passes throughout. The expected cost is about 0.06 dollars for a one-tier draft and 0.10 for a three-tier draft (D66's size and token estimates, input at 2.00 and output at 10.00 dollars per million, medium effort), so about 0.6 dollars for the nine and about 1.2 if every draft needs its retry, which carries the draft as well. The run prints the billed prompt tokens beside the bound, which measures the real characters-per-token ratio D66 assumed. Nothing in this commit runs the script live; Austin runs it after the commit.
- Evidence: the suite of 4,805 passing tests and the 3 known failures, and 142 scripted mutations, each restored byte for byte and each caught by a named test: one edit in each paragraph of the prompt and in its form (a semicolon, a non-ASCII quote, a digit outside the limits, the paragraph separator, a limit, a table entry, a count name), each of the two turns, the section table, each field of the request and of the retry request, the single retry and its turns in order, each check and each failure mode, the guards (the SDK, the adapter, the server, the environment, a print and a clock imported or used), the exemption of the memo guard, the gate and each exit code of the script, its budget, its scenario, the meter, the capture, the load-back and the recording, each figure it prints and the echo of a draft, the key and a typed error, and the report and the errors.
- Options considered: the writer step inside the memo package (rejected: the memo is pure and guarded against the model interface); a retry that resends only the findings without the draft (rejected: the writer could not correct what it cannot see); several retries (rejected: each costs a call and the first live run measures how often one is needed); reusing the live script's seam names (rejected: the guard of D62 requires them in one file); a third file for the script's helpers (rejected: scripts import each other only by path trickery, and the script fits in one file).
- Why: the writer is the one place free text enters the memo, so its prompt is fixed word for word, its request is fixed field by field, its draft is read by code before anyone sees it, and its failures are typed so that none can be mistaken for a memo.
- Revisit if: the first live run shows the retry is needed often, a rule fires on correct prose (commit four classifies each firing), the billed prompt tokens are far from the assumed ratio, or a recorded draft names a fact in a section the table does not allow.

### D67: The checker (second of four narrative commits)
- Date: 2026-11-21
- Decision: A draft is a memo only if `check_narrative(narrative, view)` returns nothing. It reads a narrative the model has already validated and the view the writer was shown, and returns findings in a fixed order: claim by claim in reading order, the rules of one claim in the order below, one finding per rule per claim, then the primary facts no claim references. A finding is a rule, a section and a claim number, plus, for an unreferenced primary fact only, that fact's id, which comes from the view and not from the draft. Each rule has one fixed message, no two alike, so no finding, line or message ever holds any of the draft, and a rejected draft can go back to the writer and into a log without carrying its text. A test puts a canary in the text and the references of a draft that fires seven rules and requires it in no finding, line or serialized form; a mutation that adds the draft's text to a finding is caught by it.
- The rules, in order: a reference or basis id that is not in the view (a fact the view trimmed is unresolvable, shown with a branch condition whose value is unknown); a placeholder whose id is not in the view; a placeholder whose id is not among the claim's references; a field outside the allowlist of its kind; a brace left in the prose; a digit; a quantity word; a claim with no reference; an inference with no basis; a fact with a basis; a recommendation phrase; a prediction phrase; text that is not plain; a reference that does not belong in its section; and, last, a primary fact that no claim references. The placeholder pattern removes a placeholder, digits and all, before the digit scan, so a made-up `{12000000:amount}` would pass the digit rule; it fails the second rule because its id is not in the view, and a test names exactly that case. Coverage of primary facts counts a reference from any claim; the section a fact must appear in is enforced by the section-kind rule and reported with the finding.
- The number rule is any character of Unicode category N (decimal digits of every script, superscripts, vulgar fractions, circled digits, Roman numeral signs), tested on the prose with placeholders removed, on the text as written and after NFKC, because NFKC turns a Roman numeral sign into ASCII letters. Tests cover Arabic-Indic and full-width digits, a superscript, a vulgar fraction and a Roman numeral sign. A company name with digits passes when it appears only inside a placeholder. The quantity words are matched as whole words after the same normalization (NFKC, lower case, apostrophes removed, hyphen, underscore and slash read as spaces, punctuation read as spaces, whitespace collapsed): zero, two to twenty, thirty to ninety by tens, hundred, thousand, million, billion, trillion, dozen, percent, half, halves, halved, halving, twice, thrice, double, doubled, doubling, triple, tripled and tripling, then the plurals hundreds, thousands, millions, billions, trillions and dozens, 52 words, pinned as a literal. Two more forms are reported as quantity words: a number word joined to fold (twofold, threefold, tenfold, hundredfold; not manifold, which is not a number word plus fold), and a fraction written in words, six phrases pinned as a literal and matched like the phrase lists: a third of, a quarter of, a fifth of, one third, one quarter and one fifth. Not banned: one, first, second, third, none, each, both, several, single, many, few, any, so the third rule, the third quarter, fiscal quarter, quarterly and manifold pass, and the first half fires because half is listed.
- The recommendation phrases are 38, pinned as a literal: buy rating, sell rating, hold rating, strong buy, strong sell, we recommend, i recommend, recommend buying, recommend selling, recommend holding, investors should, you should, worth buying, worth selling, good buy, good investment, bad investment, price target, target price, outperform, underperform, overweight, underweight, buy the stock, sell the stock, buy shares, sell shares, buy now, sell now, undervalued, overvalued, consider buying, consider selling, should buy, should sell, entry point, avoid the stock and avoid the shares. The last nine were added after the architect's probes found advice that passed ("looks undervalued", "consider buying", "One should buy", "an attractive entry point", "Avoid the shares"). `should hold` is still not in the list because it fired on technical prose in the probe of the plan ("it should hold on any platform"), and neither are `a buy` or `is a buy`, because "buy-in" reads as "buy in" and would fire; the advice sense of hold is caught by the anchored `investors should` and `you should`. `should buy` will also fire on a company that should buy back shares; the classification in commit four decides whether that is a false positive worth keeping. The prediction rule is separate and is windowed, not a list of fixed phrases, because the architect's probes showed that a fixed phrase misses an adverb or another verb ("is going to be delisted", "Nasdaq will delist the company", "Delisting is certain", "will almost certainly lose its listing" all passed). On the normalized words, it fires when a certainty marker is followed, among the next four words, by the start of a delisting term, or when a delisting term is followed, among the next three words, by certain or inevitable. The markers, pinned as a literal, are will, wont (the apostrophe is removed), going to, certain, certainly, inevitable, inevitably, sure to, bound to, expected to, likely to and unlikely to; the terms are delist, delisted, delisting, lose its listing and removed from; the followers are certain and inevitable. Two entries go beyond the architect's list so that the six fixed phrases this rule replaced still fire: likely to and unlikely to are markers, and removed from stands where removed from nasdaq was proposed, because "will be removed from the exchange" must fire. A word that is both marker and adjective, such as certain in "certain companies face delisting", will fire; commit four measures it. The window sizes are pinned and each is tested at its edge (the fourth word fires, the fifth does not; the third fires, the fourth does not). What is reported is the matched list entries, never the draft. Hedged risk language must pass, and tests show that it does: delisting risk, the risk of delisting, may receive a notice, could face delisting proceedings, Nasdaq may begin delisting proceedings, the rules that govern delisting, could be delisted, may be delisted, is likely to receive a notice, was removed from the index. The lists are a backstop behind the prompt and not a guarantee: the architect's probes of this staging found wording that still passes ("Delisting looks all but inevitable", "It won't keep its listing for long", "Expect a delisting", "a strong candidate to buy", "It seems cheap relative to peers"), and these five are inputs to the Phase 5 evaluation case that tries to draw out a buy or sell view or a prediction, rather than more list entries. Ordinary language that contains a list word must pass too: holding company, selling shareholders, shareholders, threshold, sell-side analysts, the company holds a license, buyback, it cannot be sold, the rule holds, household, overweighted by design. All the lists are provisional until commit four classifies the firings in the recorded drafts as true or false positives.
- Plain text is a character from space to tilde only, no `<`, `>` or backtick, and no `http` in any case, so a long dash, a curly quote, markup, a newline or a link in a text is a finding, tested on the raw text. The limits and the shape that the narrative model already holds are findings too, because the writer step needs to send a draft back that did not even build: `findings_from_validation` maps each error of a Pydantic `ValidationError`, by its type, its location and the fixed words of the model's own limit errors, to a fixed rule (text too long, text empty, too many placeholders, references or basis ids, claims, draft too long, an unusable reference or no reference, or shape) at the section and claim of the error, and keeps nothing of the input; an error it does not know is shape, and it never raises.
- Evidence: the architect's probes of the first staging (three gaps in the lists, closed here with a test for each addition and each edge of each window), the plan's probe of the rules over 81 texts of repository prose, the hand-written Xiao-I drafts (one passing, one failing per rule, with real ids from the recorded set), a draft with one claim for each primary fact that passes for every one of the 42 recorded sets (255 primary facts in all) and, for three of them, fails with exactly the missing fact when any one is left out, and 185 scripted mutations (each rule removed, each list entry dropped one at a time (the quantity words, the fraction phrases, the recommendation phrases, and the prediction markers, terms and followers), each window made smaller and larger by one, the fold rule removed and widened to any word ending in fold, a many-word entry matched on its first word only, NFKC removed in both places, each boundary character and the apostrophe step removed, the number test narrowed to ASCII digits and to decimal digits, the section-kind table and the count routing widened, primary coverage dropped and narrowed, the order reversed, each limit mapping swapped, a finding that echoes the draft, and two rules sharing a message), each restored byte for byte and each caught by a named test.
- Options considered: findings that quote the offending text (rejected: they would carry the draft into a retry and a log, and a prompt-injection string with it); counting digits on the raw text (rejected: a placeholder holds the value, and a company name may hold a digit); one list for recommendations and predictions (rejected: the prediction list is a separate measurement and a separate decision); banning `should hold` and its siblings (rejected on the probe's false positive); leaving the limits to the model's own errors (rejected: the writer step would have to parse the library's messages to retry).
- Why: the value of the memo is that a figure, a status or a claim in it is there because code put it there, and a checker whose findings repeat the draft, or whose number rule has a hole, would be the one place free text gets back in.
- Revisit if: the recorded drafts show a rule firing on correct prose (commit four classifies each firing), a hole in the number or quantity rules appears in a recorded draft, or the section-kind table disagrees with what the writer reasonably says.

### D66: The writer's view, the narrative schema and the projector (first of four narrative commits)
- Date: 2026-11-21
- Decision: The writer sees a view of the fact set built from ids, kinds and values only: one JSON object per fact in reading order, `{"id","kind","value"}`, with no citation, no citation id, no `rests_on`, no order, no lead mark, no type tag and no run context. The lead mark and the type tag are functions of the kind (and of a count's name), so they are left out and the prompt will name the primary kinds. Nothing in a value depends on a citation, so a recorded draft replays on any day: tests build the view on two clock days and after re-stamping the directory and compare the bytes. Two bulky kinds are trimmed: an unmeasured rule keeps its section, tier, first reason and missing inputs, and a branch condition whose value is unknown is left out, because its standard and its unmeasured rules already say so (a branch that is true or false is kept). Every primary fact (the signal, its caveat, the findings and the breached-findings count) is shown whole, and a test compares each to its fact. Measured over the seven recorded companies at 20 filings, the view is 12,016 to 15,602 bytes for one tier and 24,738 to 35,782 for three, against 27,254 to 28,869 and 70,666 to 72,531 untrimmed, which is 47 and 51 percent below at the medians. Not-measured facts and conditions were 55 to 68 percent of the untrimmed view. The token cost is not measured: D63 billed 1,714 prompt tokens for 4,559 characters of English plus a short schema, about 2.7 characters per token, and the plan assumes that ratio (4.4k to 5.8k tokens for one tier, 9.2k to 13.3k for three, plus about 1,300 for the system prompt) with 3.5 as the other end; the first live drafting run prints the billed tokens beside the bound.
- The narrative is seven fixed sections of claims, in the reading order of D54: reported_financials, findings, standards, not_measured, exchange_status, filings and verdict. A claim has a kind (fact or inference), a text, the ids of the facts it is about (never empty) and a basis (empty for a fact; for an inference the ids it rests on, which the checker will require). Values enter the text only through placeholders written `{fact-id:field}`; one regular expression defines them, and a brace left over after they are removed is malformed text. The field must be in a per-kind allowlist pinned as a literal, and each allowed field is one the view shows for that kind: a test reads the field names of every fact of every kind in all recorded views and requires the allowlist to be a subset. That test found the reason for two changes to the plan's lists: an unmeasured rule's threshold is trimmed from the view, so it is not an allowed field, and a figure's period is one field (`period`), because the view holds a period object and not the separate dates the plan named. The two caveat kinds have no field. The tables that route a fact to a section (the kinds each section may be about, and the section a count belongs to by its name) and the section each primary fact must be referenced in are code and are pinned by tests; the checker (the next commit) enforces them.
- The limits fit the data, and the model holds all of them, because the provider's subset cannot express any: lengths count the prose of a text with its placeholders removed (a long id would otherwise use up the text), placeholders have their own cap, and the draft totals are checked on the whole narrative. Computed from all 42 recorded sets (7 companies, 6 listing questions): the most primary facts any one section must cover is 17 (the findings section of Luckin's three-tier set: 14 findings and 3 breached counts), the reported_financials section needs at most 6, a whole draft must reference at most 23, and the longest placeholder is 109 characters (a condition id with `standard_name`).
