The chosen limits keep at least half as much again as headroom over those figures, except the raw-text cap, which is 1.46 times the most a claim can need at the other limits: 400 characters of prose and 12 placeholders per claim, 2,500 characters of raw text (1,708 at the limits, with twelve of the longest placeholders), 30 references and 30 basis ids per claim, 30 claims per section, 100 claims and 24,000 characters of prose per draft. A test builds, for every recorded set and in particular the largest, a draft with one claim for each primary fact, using realistic placeholders, and requires it to validate and to cover every primary fact; another puts all 17 findings into one claim and 12 of the longest placeholders into another. The prompt of the third commit will state every limit to the writer, and the plain-ASCII rule the checker will enforce.
- The projector (`llm/projector.py`), owed to this commit by D57, turns a strict Pydantic model into the supported subset: it keeps the keywords of the allowlist, reads a map of names (`properties`, `$defs`) as names and never as keywords, drops what the subset refuses (`pattern`, `minLength`, `maxLength`, `maxItems`, `minimum`, a `minItems` above one, a format outside the list, annotations such as `examples`), reports each drop with its path and value, and checks the result with the subset check before returning it. The narrative projects to 1,058 bytes with no optional property and no union node (the limits are 24 and 16), and seven drops: the length and pattern limits of the text, the ids and the counts. A test requires that every one of those drops is a limit the model enforces, each shown by a violation the model refuses.
- This commit also fixes the two defects behind D57's owed tests. `ResponseFormat.from_schema` caught every `ValueError`, so a schema that contained itself was refused with "a number in the schema is not finite", and a schema holding a set or other value that is not JSON escaped as a raw `TypeError`. A loop now says the schema refers to itself, a value that is not JSON says so, deep nesting says so, and a number that is not finite still says so, each as `InputValidationError` with the field and the reason.
- Evidence: the probes of the plan (the view sizes and each trimming option over the seven companies, the prototype projection, the provisional rules over 81 texts of repository prose, in which the recommendation list falsely fired on "it should hold on any platform", which is why the next commit anchors that phrase) and 65 scripted mutations (the projector's keyword, format and minItems rules, each place it walks, its reports and its final check; the two refusals; the view's tag, trimming, ordering, primary and leak rules; every limit by one; prose against raw counting; the sections, the routing tables, the grammar and the field allowlist; and the guards), each restored byte for byte and each caught by a named test.
- Options considered: citations or the lead mark in the view (rejected: D53, and the lead mark is a function of the kind); the untrimmed view (28 KB and 72 KB); a free-form narrative (rejected: nothing to check); limits counted on raw text (rejected: a long id would eat the prose); the projector inside the narrative module (rejected: it is generic and the `llm` package already holds the subset).
- Why: the writer's input has to be small enough to read, free of everything that changes between runs, and shaped so that a draft can be checked without reading it as prose.
- Revisit if: the first live run shows the view's token cost far from the assumed ratio, the writer misuses a placeholder field, or a recorded set needs more primary facts than the limits allow.

### D65: The memo fact builders (second of two memo commits)
- Date: 2026-11-18
- Decision: `memo/` gains the code that turns the four tool results into the fact set of D64, and nothing that writes prose, renders or calls a model. `parse_tool_result(tool, text)` reads the tool result's text content, the server's own `model_dump_json` (D47), in JSON mode only, and refuses with `ToolResultRejected(tool, field, detail)` (a new typed error in the memo family of `core/errors`, which D64 had planned and 8a had not yet needed): a tool that is not one of the four, a payload over 1,000,000 bytes (the largest recorded result is under 80,000), text that is not valid JSON or is nested too deeply, trailing data, an unexpected or missing field, or a wrong type. The field it names is a field of the tool's model or `<payload>`, and the detail is one of a few fixed sentences; nothing of the input is echoed, the error has no cause and its context is suppressed, and a test plants a canary in an unknown field and in a value and finds it in no part of the error. `ToolResults` bundles the four results of one run and refuses a result with a scope note other than the pinned one, a result about another company, a check result and an extract result that disagree on whether XBRL facts exist, a check result that lists a tier twice, lists tiers other than the one asked for, or whose Nasdaq status rows do not match its matched tickers. `build_fact_set(results)` returns the `FactSet` and the `RunContext` together and is a pure function: no clock, no I/O, no model.
- The builders. Company facts are the submissions record of the CIK: its number, name and entity type, and one fact per distinct ticker and per distinct exchange in the order first seen (the SEC lists Nasdaq once per ticker, so Credit Suisse's three Nasdaq lines are one fact; a value that cannot be an id, or is not printable ASCII of 1 to 200 characters, fails loud). Each filing of the window is a fact cited to its row (accession, form, filed date and the tool's own index page). The data status, the fiscal-year anchor (absent for GE and Credit Suisse, and then no fact), each figure with its translation flag, its reported-currency twins, its stand-in basis and the kinds of its disagreements (their detail is dropped, as D64 ruled), and each gap with its reason are cited as D64 says. A finding takes its tier from the tier view it sits in, never from a rule id, and links the figure it measured by finding the extracted figure whose citation it cites; a finding whose figure is not among the extracted ones fails loud. A rule that could not be measured cites its rule and, when a reason is an absent figure, the companyfacts coverage record. The conditions are the standard-level and branch-level states of the tools' tree (the common requirements have no standard), each resting on the rule facts of its rules. The reported_financials signal, its caveat, the verdict and its caveat rest on every rule fact of the tier. A tier with no rule in force has no facts, and the run context names it. A Nasdaq status fact carries the exchange's category, tier, financial status as a slug and, joined from the matched rows by symbol, whether Nasdaq flags the row as an ETF.
- The counts are those of D64 with the populations of A4: the filing counts of the window (returned, and one per form), the filing list's own numbers (total matching and omitted), the figures, gaps and fiscal years with figures, and per tier the findings by state, the rules not measured and the standards by value. A count whose population is empty is not emitted: a window with no filings has no returned count and no form counts, and still has its two list counts, which cite the filing list record. The reading order is set by rank, not by the order the facts are built in: company, filings, data status, fiscal year, figures, gaps, then per tier in the tools' order the signal, the caveat, the findings, the conditions, the rules not measured, the verdict and its caveat, then the exchange's status, then the counts.
- The run context holds what belongs to the run and not to the facts: the date the result was evaluated and the date the rules were chosen for (the check result's own, D46, so a run that crosses midnight UTC has one rules date; a test gives the other results another day), the listing type, pathway, tier asked for, how the tier was chosen, the tiers with no rule in force, the form filter, the number of test issues ignored, and the directory's availability, stamp and hash. The writer never sees it. Across the two clock days of the tests (2026-10-31 and 2026-11-18) over the same fixtures the 42 recorded fact sets are byte for byte identical, with equal digests, and the run contexts differ only in the two dates. A re-stamped directory changes only the directory citation's entry in the table and the citation-id list of the Nasdaq status fact, and `stable_part_json` is identical.
- Evidence. The inventory is executable: every one of the 232 fields of the four results and their nested views has a line in a table saying whether it becomes part of a fact, a citation, the run context, a check, or is dropped with its reason, and the test fails when a field is added without a line or a line names a field that is gone. Every one of the 70 recorded results (7 companies, 10 calls) parses and re-dumps byte for byte. Every one of the 42 recorded sets (7 companies, 6 listing questions) builds under all the validators of D64. The hand oracles are typed from the raw fixtures (companyfacts, submissions, the Nasdaq file and the ruleset YAML), never from the builders: Xiao-I (three breaches with their arithmetic headroom, two gaps, three cross-tag marks, the directory's deficient status, 20 filings with 108 matching and 88 omitted), Luckin (every figure a translation with its yuan twin and the implied rate as the exact quotient, the stand-in by identity, two clear translated findings), Credit Suisse (three exchange lines in one fact, three ETF rows, no tier assessed), Trans American (an initial listing in three tiers, capital breached, hand counts per tier, no gap), GE (no fiscal-year anchor, six gaps, absent figures citing the coverage record) and a company the SEC answers 404 for (the no-facts status, zero counts citing the coverage record). Three values were first taken from a probe of the builders and not from the raw files (Trans American's per-tier counts of breached findings and unmeasured rules, and GE's list of absent concepts); the architect re-derived all three from the recorded tool results with plain JSON parsing, independently of the memo code, and they match. The tier of every one of the 777 rule facts agrees with its standard's tier and with the tier letter in its rule id. The golden set (Xiao-I, continued, tier from the directory, 20 filings: 96 facts, 50 citations, 57,208 canonical bytes) is pinned by its digest and its count per kind, with sixteen facts pinned as literals with the citations they resolve to. 75 scripted mutations (the parser's strictness, size limit, field echo and error chain; each check of the bundle; each field of the run context; the collapse of tickers and exchanges; a filing's dates; each part of a figure; the tier fill, a finding's measured value, headroom, state, translation flag, figure link and citations; the reasons' order; the coverage citation; the conditions; the caveats and verdict; what the derived facts rest on; the tier order; the ETF mark and status slug; every count; the empty-population rule; the reading order; and the guards) were each restored byte for byte and each caught by a named test (a seventy-sixth, counting tier counts for a tier with no rules, was dropped as an equivalent mutant: the empty-population rule already emits nothing for it).
- Sizes at 20 filings, per recorded company, for one tier (continued, Global Select named) and for three (initial, all tiers): facts, citations, canonical bytes of the whole set, and the bytes of the citation-free part (`stable_part_json`) from which the writer's view will be built. The citation-free part is about 64 percent of a set's bytes (36 KB of 58 KB for one tier, 91 KB of 142 KB for three), most of it the lists of the facts that each derived fact and count rests on, which the writer's view will not show; that view will show only id, kind and value, and the next commit decides which fields of the bulky kinds (the unmeasured rules and the conditions) it shows. The prototype of D64 put the writer's view at 24 KB for one tier and 62 KB for three.
| Company | One tier: facts | citations | canonical bytes | citation-free bytes | Three tiers: facts | citations | canonical bytes | citation-free bytes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| trans_american | 95 | 50 | 57,651 | 36,146 | 195 | 88 | 141,728 | 91,755 |
| luckin | 96 | 48 | 58,027 | 36,554 | 196 | 86 | 142,282 | 92,257 |
| ge | 97 | 44 | 55,199 | 35,301 | 197 | 82 | 138,659 | 90,398 |
| aixi | 96 | 50 | 58,002 | 36,114 | 196 | 88 | 141,835 | 91,458 |
| aacg | 97 | 51 | 58,952 | 37,012 | 197 | 89 | 142,337 | 92,475 |
| credit_suisse | 98 | 45 | 55,980 | 35,432 | 198 | 83 | 139,626 | 90,484 |
| agmh | 98 | 50 | 58,216 | 36,373 | 198 | 88 | 141,719 | 91,744 |
- Options considered: building the facts in the order they are read (rejected: a derived fact needs the citations of the facts it rests on, so rank sets the order afterwards); a lax parse of the structured dictionary (rejected in D64); reading a tier from a rule id (rejected: the tier comes from the view and a test checks it against the rule id's letter); keeping an unmeasured rule's detail sentence (rejected: it carries tag names); a fact for a tier with no rule in force (rejected: there is nothing to cite); emitting a count with an empty population (rejected by A4).
- Why: the facts layer is where code decides every figure, status and citation, so each rule of the schema is exercised by real results from every recorded company and by hand oracles that do not share the builders' code, and what changes with the day or the hour is kept apart in the run context.
- Revisit if: the writer's view needs the disagreement detail or a smaller view of the bulky kinds, a tool result gains a field (the inventory fails until it is decided), a company with a non-ASCII name appears (it fails loud), or the multi-year series changes how figures are keyed.

### D64: The memo fact schema (first of two memo commits; the builders are the second)
- Date: 2026-11-18
- Decision: `memo/` is a new pure package: it imports the standard library, pydantic, `core`, `domain` and only the tools' result models (`echo`, `numbers` and the six `models_` modules), never the service, the server, the model interface, ingestion, extraction, the engines or an HTTP library, and it reads no clock, no randomness and no environment variable and prints nothing (a guard with self-tests, as for `llm/`). This commit builds the schema; nothing in it builds a fact from a tool result, writes prose, renders or calls a model. A fact set is a sorted tuple of facts and a table of citations. A fact has a stable id, one of 15 closed kinds (company, filing, data_status, fiscal_year, figure, gap, nasdaq_status, finding, not_measured, condition_status, reported_financials, financial_caveat, verdict, verdict_caveat, count), a lead mark, its zero-based place in the tools' published reading order, one typed value, at least one citation id, and, for a derived fact or a count, the ids of the facts it rests on. The lead mark is primary for reported_financials, its caveat, the findings and the breached-findings count, secondary for the verdict and its caveat, and context for the rest, so the order the tools publish (D54) survives. The value of each kind is its own frozen, strict model, closed to extra fields and tagged by a `type` equal to the kind; it holds exact fixed-point text for numbers (the D45 form, scale kept, never a float), dates, integers, booleans, members of closed vocabularies, ids, ruleset labels and bounded identity texts, and never filing text, a detail sentence, a tag name, a URL or a security name. An identity text from SEC metadata (a name, a ticker, a form type) is printable ASCII of 1 to 200 characters with no space at either end, else `IdentityRefused`: it is data and not instructions, and it is refused, never cleaned. The vocabularies are copies of the engine's enums written as Literals, each pinned equal to its source by a test, so a new engine member fails until it is decided here. Ruleset labels (a rule section, a standard name) are bounded ASCII patterns, since they are ours and not filing text.
- Ids are lower-case ASCII slugs built from the fact's own content and checked against it by the schema (a fact whose id its value does not call for is refused): `company.ticker.aixi`, `filing.<accession>`, `fig.stockholders_equity.2025-12-31`, `find.<rule id>`, `cond.<tier>.<standard>.<requirement>.<branch>`, `tier.global.verdict`, `count.<name>`. In the recorded results each of the 102 rule ids occurs in exactly one listing type and tier, so an id does not depend on how many tiers were checked. A slug turns a slash into an underscore and a space into a hyphen and refuses any other character; two texts that still meet in one slug meet the registry, which refuses the second (`IdCollision`). The SEC lists one exchange several times (Credit Suisse lists Nasdaq three times, once per ticker), so builders must collapse equal values.
- Citations. Gaps, an absent figure, company identity, filing rows and the fiscal-year anchor had no locator, and the domain `Citation` union is referenced by no published schema (so extending it would change no tool schema and no size ceiling) but nests its identifiers (`{"cik":{"value":..}}`, a bare accession) and has no rule kind. So the memo owns a flat union of four kinds and the domain union stays at three; this amends D5, D26 and D43 in words: the fourth kind exists, in the memo. The kinds: the tools' XBRL citation tagged `xbrl`, the tools' directory citation, the rule citation (section, url, quoted text, date read, release), and a new `sec_record` kind for the SEC's own records that are not an XBRL fact: a field of the submissions record (cik, name, entity type, tickers, exchanges), one filing row of it (accession, form, filed date, index page), the filing list, or the companyfacts record (its coverage, or the fiscal-year anchor with its accession and form). The filing parts are present exactly for the fields that name a filing. This also settles the submissions field without an accession that D53 left for the flags pass, because company facts need it now. Each kind of fact must carry the citation kinds it is allowed (a company fact cites a record, a figure its XBRL fact, a finding its rule and, when it measured a filing fact, that fact, a Nasdaq status the directory row), and the citation must agree with the value where it can (a filing's accession, form and date, a figure's unit and period, a company's field). A derived fact or a count cites exactly the union of what it rests on. The fact set holds each citation once under `c.` and the first 16 hex characters of the SHA-256 of its canonical JSON; a table that holds two different citations with one id is refused (`IdCollision`), never one kept silently, and a test patches the hash to collide. A finding links the figure it measured by id, and the figure must be the one whose citation it cites.
- Stability. Citation ids hash content that includes the directory's stamp and file hash, which change hourly in a live run, so citation ids and the fact set's digest are stable only over fixed inputs. A fact's id, kind, lead, order and value do not depend on them, and a test re-stamps the directory (a new stamp and hash over the same facts) and proves that only the citation table entry and the citation id list of the Nasdaq status fact change. `stable_part_json` is the part of every fact that hashes no citation; the writer's view (the next commit) must be built from it and must exclude citation ids and everything else that hashes run-dependent content. The day does not enter a fact at all: across two days (2026-10-31 and 2026-11-18) over the same fixtures the only value that differs in any of the 56 recorded results is `evaluated_on`, and the prototype's fact sets were identical in 28 of 28 cases. `evaluated_on`, the date the rules were chosen for, the directory stamp and the choices of the call belong to a separate run context (the next commit) that the writer never sees.
- The input. The parser (next commit) takes the tool result's text content, the server's own `model_dump_json` (D47), not the structured-content dictionary. The result models are strict, so a decoded dictionary fails validation in all 56 recorded results (dates, tuples and enums), while JSON mode passes all 56 and re-serializes them byte for byte; tests pin both. No recorded result contains a float literal (a test parses every one with a hook that refuses floats), so the structured route would be lossless too, but the text is the contract.
- Counts. A count names the fact ids it counts (`rests_on`), is the number of them (except for three counts that restate a tool's number or a label and count no fact: the total matching filings, the omitted filings and the fiscal years with figures), and cites the union of their citations. When it counts none (a zero) it cites its population, and the population of every count is fixed in a table in the code: the filing counts of the window have the filing facts, the filing list's own counts have the submissions record's list, the counts of figures, gaps and fiscal years have the companyfacts record, and every count of a tier has the tier's rule facts (a tier with rules in force always has them). A count whose population is empty is not emitted, because no fact may exist without a citation; the helper returns nothing for it. A count with no defined population is refused.
- Review fixes. The citation table holds only cited citations: a table entry that no fact cites is refused, naming its id. A finding and a not_measured fact carry their tier (a member of the Tier vocabulary), because the tier was otherwise only inside a rule id; fact ids do not change, a derived fact (a condition, a signal, a caveat, a verdict) may rest only on rule facts of its own tier, and a rule fact whose standard has a standard-level condition in the set must have that condition's tier. The schema, and not only the helper the builders call, enforces the count populations: a count that rests on facts may rest only on members of its population (filing facts for the filing counts, and for a form count those of that form; figure facts for the figures; gap facts for the gaps; for a count named tier.<t>.<rest>, with t read from the name and checked against the Tier vocabulary, the findings of tier t in the state the name gives, the not_measured facts of tier t, or, for the standards counts, the standard-level conditions of tier t with the value the name gives, which rest on those rule facts); a count that rests on nothing, a zero or a count of a tool's number or a label, cites exactly the union of the citations of its whole population (every filing fact of the window; the submissions citation whose field is filings; the companyfacts citation whose field is concept_coverage; the finding and not_measured facts of tier t); and a count whose population is empty, or that the set does not hold the population for, cannot exist in a set. The first draft let a label count cite the Nasdaq directory and a zero count cite the same, and accepted both.
- Policy. The complete field set of every memo model is pinned as an exact literal, an allow-list that fails closed, beside a walk over every field name, class name, kind and vocabulary member for the banned words recommend, recommendation, rating, buy, sell, hold, advice, upgrade, downgrade, outperform, underperform, overweight, underweight and the pairs price target and target price, matched as whole tokens (so threshold, holders and implied_rate pass; verdict, signal and state are the engine's listing-standard words and are allowed). So no field can hold a recommendation, a rating or a price target; the existing scope note stays word for word and is the renderer's.
- Evidence: the prototype over the 7 recorded companies (a one-tier set is 95 to 98 facts and 49 to 52.5 KB with a citation table, against 108 to 116 KB with citations inline; three tiers 195 to 198 facts and 123 to 127 KB against 334 to 363 KB; the writer's view of id, kind and value is 23.6 to 25.1 KB and 61.3 to 63.0 KB, against tool results of 21.0 to 23.9 KB and 72.1 to 77.6 KB), the hand-built Xiao-I set of the tests (every kind, written from the prototype's output with no builder), the vocabulary pins against the engine, every validator of the fact and the set refused by an edit that only it can catch, and 107 scripted mutations, each restored byte for byte and each caught by a named test.
- Options considered: citations inline in each fact (2.2 to 2.9 times larger, and each rule's quoted text repeated); extending the domain union (no consumer, nested shapes, no rule kind); a lax parser over the structured dictionary (it would accept coercions the strict models refuse); a decimal type in the values (the fixed-point text is lossless in JSON and needs no serializer); free-text details in gaps and reasons (they carry tag names); one commit for schema and builders (about 1,400 source lines).
- Why: code decides every figure, status and citation, and the writer will see fact ids, kinds and values only, so the schema has to make a fact without a citation, a value that is text from a filing, and an id that drifts from its content impossible to build, and has to keep what is stable apart from what changes every hour.
- Revisit if: the writer needs a smaller view (the not_measured and condition_status facts are the bulk of it), a reader fact needs a flat text citation, the multi-year series changes how figures are keyed, or the disagreement detail of a figure (eight cross-tag cases and no restatement in the recorded data are carried as kinds only) is needed.

### D63: The first live run and its recordings
- Date: 2026-11-17
- Decision: Austin ran `scripts/live_llm.py` in his own terminal on 2026-11-17 and it exited 0. The four recordings and their manifest it wrote are committed exactly as written, in `tests/fixtures/llm/live_format_proof/`, as the first live scenario (15,904 bytes in all, well under the 10 MiB ceiling of D60). Git stores them as exact bytes (the `-text` attribute), the existing manifest and recording tests cover the folder as soon as it exists, and a new test replays the scenario through the fake client and a `Meter` with the shipped price table and the script's budget, in the run's order, against hand-typed figures.
- The run, call by call. One, `live.haiku_structured` (fingerprint `d3d244762286`): Haiku 5.5, input 312, output 47, no cache, thinking 0, cost 0.000054700, worst case 0.00062000, bound 600, billed prompt 312, margin 288. Two, `live.sonnet_structured` (`76c5e1007ed8`): Sonnet 5.5, the same usage, cost 0.00109400, worst case 0.01240000, bound 600, billed prompt 312, margin 288. Three, `live.cache_write` (`3a3e35ebd3c1`): Haiku 5.5, input 22, output 9, a five-minute cache write of 1,692, cost 0.000218200, worst case 0.00092840, bound 2,142, billed prompt 1,714, margin 428. Four, `live.cache_read` (`b4f28b74cde9`): Haiku 5.5, input 22, output 9, cache read 1,692, cost 0.000023620, worst case 0.00092840, bound 2,142, billed prompt 1,714, margin 428. Totals: four attempts, 4,164 tokens, 0.001390520 dollars, no failed call, price table version 1. No proxy variable was set, and `service_tier` and `speed` were standard or absent on every call.
- What the run settles. First, the served model equals the requested id for both Haiku 5.5 and Sonnet 5.5 on all four calls, which closes the check owed since D58 and D61 (the meter's mismatch rule never fired and has never been needed). Second, the token-counting endpoint's count equaled the billed prompt tokens on all four calls, with the structured-output configuration in the count request, so the bound policy of D61 (ten percent plus 256 tokens) was pure headroom here: margins of 288 and 428 tokens are exactly the pad and the percentage on counts of 312 and 1,714 (ceil of 1.10 times 312 is 344 and of 1.10 times 1,714 is 1,886, plus 256). The margin is kept unchanged, because four calls on two prompts are too few to justify lowering a safety margin whose failure closes the meter. Third, the cache split maps correctly live: a five-minute write of 1,692 tokens on the first cache call and a read of the same 1,692 on the second, with nothing in the one-hour line, and the run reported that the second Haiku call read what the first wrote. Fourth, thinking was zero on both models at effort low, with thinking set to adaptive and its display omitted. Fifth, every cost line equals the price table's rates applied to the usage, and the whole run cost 0.00139052 dollars against reserved worst cases summing to roughly ten times that, so the five-cent cap of D62 was far above the need. Sixth, the answers were correct (the listing question true with the right quote, 2 plus 2 equals 4 true, 3 plus 3 equals 7 false); that is a sanity check of the path, not an evaluation of anything.
- Still pending. The printed total of 0.00139052 dollars is to be compared with the cost the Console reports for the ListingLens workspace once it appears there; that comparison is the end-to-end check D62 moved to the bill, and it will be recorded when Austin reads it. Until then the price table's rates are verified against the documentation (D58) and against the run's arithmetic, and not against an invoice.
- Evidence: the four recordings and the manifest (the architect checked that all four load, that the manifest hashes match, that the files are ASCII with LF only, that nothing in them is key-shaped, and that a replay through the fake client and the meter reproduces the run's totals with nothing unused), the printed figures above, and 8 scripted mutations, each restored byte for byte and each caught by a named test of `tests/test_llm_recordings_live.py` or the manifest test: a price rate (a cost), the worst-case rule (a worst case), a recorded bound, a recorded output count (the totals), a recorded cache read, a recorded served model, a replay that leaves a recording unused, and one changed character in one recording.
- Options considered: lowering the bound's margin to the measured zero (rejected above); rewriting the recordings with a placeholder request id or text (rejected: the recordings are what the run wrote, and the loader already holds the live ones to a provider request id); asserting the figures against the price table in code instead of literals (rejected: a test that recomputes its own expected values from the code it checks cannot catch a wrong rate).
- Why: the fake client and the meter now have a scenario recorded from the real provider, so every later test of the reader and the memo writer can replay real response shapes, and the numbers the cost rules, the bound and the cache split rest on have been seen once outside a mock.
- Revisit if: the Console's cost for the workspace differs from 0.00139052 dollars, a later live run shows a count below the billed prompt, a served model other than the requested id, or nonzero thinking at effort low, or a price page change that makes these figures stale.

### D62: The gated live script (second of two commits; Austin runs it, no test does)
- Date: 2026-11-17
- Decision: `scripts/live_llm.py` makes four real model calls and writes what they returned as live recordings. It is manual only and outside the test paths. The order of refusals is fixed: if `LISTINGLENS_ALLOW_LIVE` is not exactly `1`, it exits 1 before `Settings()` is built, before the SDK is imported and before any file is touched (a test runs a fresh process and shows neither the SDK nor its HTTP library is loaded); then a command-line argument exits 2 (there are none); then `Settings()` (which also needs `SEC_EDGAR_CONTACT`; a missing contact exits 1); then no key exits 5; then a typed model error, a broken promise of the meter (D59), a budget stop, an answer that did not complete, or a recording that cannot be made or will not load back, each exits 1. A run that stops on a call writes nothing for that call and stops, so earlier calls keep their recordings and the manifest names exactly the files that are there. An answer that stopped for any reason but a normal end (cut off, refused, context full) is a stop and not a recording, because a recording of it would teach the fake client a failure as if it were a model answer; the first live run needs those cases to show up as a stop that Austin reads.
- The run. Four calls through `open_model_client` under a `Meter`, over a small capture wrapper that keeps each call's bound and response and changes nothing that passes through it, so a recording holds the bound the client really reported. One: Haiku 5.5 (`live.haiku_structured`) with the hand recording's system text, user text and schema (format name `proof_listing`), no cache lifetime, effort low. Two: Sonnet 5.5 (`live.sonnet_structured`) with the same texts and schema. Three and four: Haiku 5.5 (`live.cache_write`, then `live.cache_read`) with one system text of about 4,560 characters (a fixed sentence repeated 60 times, roughly 1,800 tokens, above the documented caching minimum), a five-minute cache lifetime, a one-boolean schema named `proof_answer`, and the users "Is 2 plus 2 equal to 4?" and "Is 3 plus 3 equal to 7?". Every call asks for at most 1,000 output tokens at effort low. The run reports whether the fourth call read the cache the third wrote (yes only when the third call's usage shows a five-minute cache write and the fourth shows cache reads) and whether any proxy variable is set (yes or no, never its value).
- The budget and the sum of worst cases. The caps are six attempts, 20,000 tokens and five cents. The sum of the four reserved worst cases (D58), at assumed counts of 150, 150, 2,000 and 2,000 prompt tokens (bounds of 421, 421, 2,456 and 2,456 by the D61 rule), is 0.0142506 dollars: 0.00058420 for the first Haiku call, 0.01168400 for Sonnet and 0.00099120 for each cache call. That is under half of five cents (0.025), so the cap stays at five cents and is not raised to ten. The reserved tokens add up to about 9,750, under half the token cap. A test recomputes the sum from the packaged price table and pins both the caps and the sum, so a change to a rate or a cap shows up.
- What it prints, and what it does not. For each call: the request fingerprint's first twelve characters, the stop, the served model, the usage figures, the exact cost, the worst case, the bound, the billed prompt tokens and the bound's margin over them (this is the measurement D61 left to the first live run), then the run's totals including the total cost in dollars. It prints `service_tier` and `speed` as "standard or absent": the adapter refuses any other value before a response exists, so that line confirms and does not measure. It never prints any request text, any response text or the key, and a failed call prints only the error's class and its message, which D61 builds from fixed words, a status, a type slug and a request id.
- `inference_geo` is not printed, and this supersedes the sentence in D61 that says the first live run prints it. The adapter stays exactly as committed in D61: it does not expose the field, `ModelResponse` and `Usage` have nowhere to carry it, and adding either would change a committed interface for a figure that no longer decides anything. The pricing question it was meant to answer is settled by the Console reading of 2026-11-17: the ListingLens workspace's inference geo Allowed is "Unrestricted" and its Default is "Global routing", and the Console notes that restricting to a specific geography is billed at 1.1 times the global rate. Calls that omit `inference_geo` therefore use global routing at standard prices and the price table needs no multiplier. The separate "Workspace geo: United States" setting governs data storage and processing such as code execution, not inference routing, and the response field reports where inference ran, which is not what triggers the 1.1 times price. The end-to-end check moves to the bill: the script prints the run's total cost, and Austin compares it with the cost the Console reports for the workspace once it appears.
- The test seam. `main` takes a keyword-only client factory and an output folder, named `model_client_factory` and `fixtures_dir`, which only tests pass; the command-line entry is exactly `main(sys.argv[1:])`. Guard tests prove that no other file under `src/` or `scripts/` names either, that the entry passes nothing else (a mutation that wires the folder to the command line is caught), that the script imports no provider or HTTP code of its own, that no workflow names the gate, that only the script's own tests name it, and that no test function both names it and starts a process. The tests call `main` with a scripted client from a temporary folder with no `.env` (because `Settings()` reads `./.env`), never run the script as a process with the gate set, and never touch the network.
- Recordings. Each completed call is made with `make_recording` as a live source, written with `write_recording`, then loaded back with `load_recording`; a file that would not load is removed before the run stops, and the manifest entry is written only after the load succeeds. The folder is `tests/fixtures/llm/live_format_proof/`. The recordings written by a scripted test replay through the fake client with nothing left unused, and their manifest passes the same checks as the shipped scenario folders.
- Evidence: the 7a adapter and meter as committed, the price table's rates, the Console reading above, and 34 scripted mutations, each restored byte for byte and each caught by a named test: the gate checked after `Settings()` or loosened, each exit code swapped, an argument check removed, a file written before the gate, response text, request text, the key or a proxy value printed, the load-back check removed or a bad file left on disk, a recording written for a broken call or for an answer that did not complete, the manifest not written, the source changed, the recorded bound changed, each budget cap and the output limit changed, the cache lifetime dropped, the models or the calls reordered, the cache report forced to yes or reading the wrong call, the proxy report forced to no, a failure that exits 0, the service line dropped, the test seam reachable from the command line, the factory ignored and the meter bypassed.
- Options considered: exposing `inference_geo` through a read-only attribute of the adapter or a field of the response (rejected above); recording every answer including cut-off ones (rejected above); checking the gate after reading settings (rejected: a refusal must touch nothing); running the script in CI against a recorded transport (rejected: the adapter already has mock-transport tests and the script's value is the live run); a command-line flag for the output folder (rejected: a recording written somewhere unreviewed is the failure the guard exists to prevent).
- Why: a first live call spends real money and puts real text on the wire, so the script is small, refuses first and touches nothing until the gate, key and contact are all present, holds the run to a cap far above its expected cost (the worst cases sum to 0.0142506 dollars, the expected cost is a fraction of a cent), and leaves behind only checked recordings and figures.
- Revisit if: the first live run shows the bound's margin too small or far too large, a served model other than the requested id, a cache that was not written or read, a bill that does not match the printed total cost (which would reopen the geography and price question), or a stop on a call that needs a different rule.

### D61: The Anthropic adapter, the key setting and the fixed host in llm/ (first of two commits; the live script is the second)
- Date: 2026-11-16
- Decision: `llm/anthropic_client.py` is the one file that imports `anthropic`, and only inside a function (its annotations use an import under `TYPE_CHECKING`), because a cold import takes about five seconds and the XBRL tools never need it; `llm/factory.py` builds it from `Settings` with `open_model_client`, which raises `ModelNotConfigured` without a key. The key is `LISTINGLENS_ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` on the core `Settings` as an optional secret: a blank value is none, there is no shape check (so a settings error never has a reason to quote it), and `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` is never read by our code. The adapter takes the secret, reveals it once into the SDK constructor with an explicit `base_url` of `https://api.anthropic.com`, `max_retries=0`, an explicit timeout, `open_telemetry=False` and a blank webhook key, and then checks, failing closed, that the client's host is the fixed one and that its default headers are exactly the twelve names the pinned SDK 2.3.0 sends (`Accept`, `Authorization`, `Content-Type`, `User-Agent`, `anthropic-version` and seven `X-Stainless-` names); an SDK upgrade that adds a header fails there. An explicit key makes the SDK skip every credential variable and the on-disk profile, and an explicit `base_url` beats `ANTHROPIC_BASE_URL`, but the SDK always merges `ANTHROPIC_CUSTOM_HEADERS` into the headers: a probe with every variable planted sent our key once, in `authorization`, to the fixed host, and also sent the two planted custom headers. So a set `ANTHROPIC_CUSTOM_HEADERS` stops construction with `ClientConfigurationRefused`, which names the header names and never their values, and the adapter does not read the environment to do it. An optional transport argument exists for tests only: it is keyword-only, named `test_http_client`, the factory never passes it, and a guard test proves that no other file under `src/` or `scripts/` names it.
- Logging. At DEBUG the SDK logs the whole request JSON, which is filing text (a probe with a canary in the system text and a message printed it once and never printed the key), and `ANTHROPIC_LOG` is read when the SDK is imported. The adapter therefore sets the `anthropic`, `httpx2` and `httpcore2` loggers to WARNING right after the import, always, even against a user's explicit `ANTHROPIC_LOG`, because the SDK has no way to log without bodies; the same probe then logged nothing. The adapter itself logs nothing.
- The network. The proxy and the trust store behave as the SEC transport's do. An HTTPS proxy only tunnels (`httpcore2` sends `CONNECT` and then TLS to the target), so the key and the prompt stay inside TLS and the proxy sees the host name; the SDK's default client honors the environment's proxies exactly as `httpx` does for the SEC transport (a probe with an unroutable proxy failed with a connection error, and a test proves the connection is attempted to the proxy and not to Anthropic), so a user whose network requires a proxy needs no extra setting. The default trust roots differ (the operating system's through `truststore` for the SDK, `certifi` for the SEC transport) and both honor `SSL_CERT_FILE` and `SSL_CERT_DIR`; pinning `certifi` here would add a third-party import and break users behind a TLS-inspecting proxy their machine trusts. The risk the SDK carries beyond the SEC transport (a secret and filing text on the wire) is the same as any browser's and needs a CA the machine trusts.
- Streaming and limits. The interface stays blocking and does not stream. D57's figure of 16,000 tokens for the SDK's non-streaming ceiling was wrong: the SDK's own rule (`_calculate_nonstreaming_timeout`) refuses above `3600 x max_tokens / 128000` seconds greater than 600, which is a `max_tokens` above 21,333, and only when no timeout is given. The adapter refuses more than 16,000 output tokens as its own policy (`RequestNotSupported`, before any request, also from the bound call) and always passes a timeout: 5 seconds to connect and, to read, 200 seconds (the documented 180-second compilation allowance of a first request with a new schema, with slack) plus the SDK's formula, capped at 600; so 1,000 tokens gets 228.125 seconds, 4,000 gets 312.5, 8,000 gets 425 and 16,000 gets 600. The reader needs under 1,000 output tokens and the memo writer about 4,000 to 8,000.
- The bound. `input_token_bound` is the token-counting endpoint's count for the same system, messages, thinking and output configuration, raised by ten percent and 256 tokens: `ceil(count x 1.10) + 256`. The endpoint is free and has its own rate limits, separate from message creation; the page lists 5,000, 10,000 and 20,000 requests per minute for the Start, Build and Scale usage tiers. The page calls the count an estimate that can differ by a small amount and may include tokens added by Anthropic that are not billed, and it does not say whether `output_config.format` (the schema) is counted, so the count request carries the same `output_config` as the real call, the margin is a stated policy, and the first live run measures the bound against the billed prompt tokens. A local bound from the byte length was rejected: about 2.5 characters per token would overstate a 250 KB section by about 2.5 times and push Haiku, which costs five times more above 100,000 tokens, into its high band. The meter still closes on a bound below the real prompt (D59), so a bad margin costs a stopped run and never an unbounded spend, and a failed counting call is a typed error that is no attempt and no charge.
- The request and the response. The request carries only the fields of D57 as a system text block (with `cache_control` and its `ttl` when a lifetime is set), text blocks for the turns, `output_config` with the JSON schema format and the effort only when the request has one, and `thinking` set explicitly to adaptive with its display omitted, so that a changed default can never start returning thinking text; nothing else is ever sent, and `inference_geo` in particular never is. A response is used only if its `service_tier` is absent or `standard` and its `speed` is absent or `standard` (an allowlist, because the SDK builds responses without checking its literals and an unknown future value would pass a blocklist), its stop is `end_turn`, `max_tokens`, `model_context_window_exceeded` or `refusal`, and its blocks are text with ignorable thinking blocks; a refusal carries no text and the provider's category; usage goes through `usage_from_counts`. `usage.inference_geo` is not refused: the data-residency page says a workspace can have a default inference geography that applies when the request omits the field (`default_inference_geo`, with `allowed_inference_geos` restricting it, and an organization that had opted out of global routing was migrated to a US-only workspace default), that the only values are `global` and `us`, that US-only inference is priced at 1.1 times on every line, and that the response field reports where inference ran; it does not say in so many words that a workspace default of `us` is priced the same as the request field, though the pricing section is written about US-only inference. The price table has no multiplier (D58), so the ListingLens workspace's data-residency setting should be read in the Console, and the first live run prints the field; a value of `us` would need a decision on a 1.1 times rule before more runs.
- Errors are a family under `ModelError` built only from fixed words, a status, a type slug that matches `[a-z_]` up to 32 characters (else "unrecognized"), and the request id from the response header (never the body): `ModelNotConfigured`, `ClientConfigurationRefused`, `RequestNotSupported`, `ProviderRejected`, `SchemaTooComplex` (a 400 whose message contains "Schema is too complex for compilation", matched and never copied), `ProviderUnavailable` (kinds rate_limited, overloaded, server_error, timeout, connection) and `UnexpectedModelResponse`. The SDK's exception holds the request and so the key, its message can quote the request (a probe showed a 400 repeating the canary), and a connection error's cause holds the request too, so each error is raised outside the handler that caught the SDK's exception and has neither a cause nor a context. `max_retries` is zero because the SDK retries 408, 409, 429, every status of 500 and above and connection errors, each retry would be a request the meter never saw, and the documentation does not say that failed requests are unbilled; the workflow decides any retry through the meter.
- Guards. `test_llm_boundaries.py` keeps its allow-list and banned names for every `llm/` file except the adapter. The adapter has its own: standard library, `pydantic`, `listinglens.core`, `listinglens.llm` and `anthropic` only, the SDK only inside a function or under `TYPE_CHECKING`, never `httpx`, `httpx2`, `httpcore2`, `requests`, `urllib3`, `aiohttp`, `socket`, `ssl`, `urllib`, `http`, `mcp`, `mcp_types` or `dotenv`, no environment read and no `print`. No other file under `src/` or `scripts/` imports the SDK, and none names `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` as a constant. The repository venv had not been synced since the dependency commit (`anthropic` was in the lock but not installed), so the build began with `uv sync --locked`, which changes the venv and not the lock.
- `.env.example` names `LISTINGLENS_ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` (its Langfuse lines belong to the tracing commit and are untouched) and the README's one line about the template names it too.
- Evidence: the probes and sources above (SDK 2.3.0 `_client.py`, `_base_client.py`, `_utils/_logs.py`, `_constants.py`, the types; `httpcore2`'s tunnel and trust code; `httpx`'s configuration), the pricing, token-counting, structured-outputs, errors and data-residency pages read on the build day, the exact request for the hand recording's request pinned in a test, and 78 scripted mutations, each restored byte for byte and each caught by a named test: every field that must never be sent, the thinking, cache and effort mapping, the retry count, the timeout and the ceiling, each stop and each block rule, the allowlist against a blocklist, the cache split and the thinking meter, the bound arithmetic and its checks, every status mapped wrongly, the error chain, the provider's text or a body request id copied, the header allowlist and host check, each of the three loggers, the adapter's representation and attributes, the key read from the general variable, a blank key kept, a shape check added, the factory passing the test argument or building without a key, and each guard rule.
- Options considered: overriding an unexpected header (rejected: an override cannot name what it removed and the SDK may add a mechanism we do not know); leaving the SDK's logger levels to the user (rejected: it would put filing text on stderr); ignoring the environment's proxies (rejected: it breaks users who need one and diverges from the SEC transport); pinning `certifi` (rejected above); streaming behind the blocking interface (rejected: nothing needs it under 16,000 tokens and D42 wants one blocking call); a local byte bound (rejected above); SDK retries (rejected above); the factory passing a transport (rejected: it is test-only and a mutation proves a guard catches it).
- Why: the key and the filing text go to one fixed host through one file whose construction, request, response and errors are checked and tested against a mock transport, so nothing about the live path needs a model call to be verified except the numbers only a live run can show, which the second commit measures.
- Revisit if: the first live run shows the bound margin too small or far too large, a served model other than the requested id, a `usage.inference_geo` of `us`, a retry policy the workflow needs, or an SDK upgrade that adds a header or changes a default.

### D60: The recorded-answer format and the fake client in llm/ (last of four llm commits; D59 is the third)
- Date: 2026-11-16
- Decision: a model answer is recorded as one JSON file, `llm-recording-v1`. It holds the fingerprint format it was made under (D57's), the fingerprint, whether a person wrote it or a live run did (`hand` or `live`), when it was recorded (UTC, to the second, written with a `+00:00` suffix only; a `Z` suffix, another offset, a missing zone and fractions of a second are refused), the input bound the client reported, the whole request as a `ModelRequest` object, and the whole response with its usage. The request is stored as an object and not as D57's canonical text because the canonical text leaves out the purpose and the format name (labels), so it cannot rebuild a request, and an object diffs readably when a stale recording has to be diagnosed. The file has no field for a header, a key, a URL or any transport detail; a test pins the field sets of the recording, the request, the response and the usage, checks that no key in any recording is named like one of those, and scans every file under `tests/fixtures/llm/` for a key shape (`sk-ant-`), an `x-api-key` header and an `authorization:` header carrying a bearer, basic or key value (the scan is proved to fire on a key built at run time and on both headers, and not to fire on filing text that says "authorization"). The CI job gitleaks scans every tracked file with its default rules, the whole history, and no allowlist, so a key-shaped string under `tests/fixtures/` would fail it and stay in history; gitleaks is not installed on the development machine, so this local test is the first guard.
- A recording is found by the request fingerprint alone, so renaming a call site never breaks a replay: one answer per fingerprint, replayed the same way every time, and a recording never holds an error (a sequence of answers or a transport failure is the scripted stub's job, D59). The loader is as strict as the price table (D58): no float, `NaN`, `Infinity` or duplicate key, no unknown field at any level, no file over 1 MiB (an estimate of four times a 100,000-token section, about 250 KB with the request stored in full, from the models page's ratio of about 2.5 characters per token; to be replaced by measurement in the reader's plan pass), no nesting too deep for the parser, and every failure is `RecordingInvalid` naming the file and the field, never echoing the request or the answer. It adds a canonical-form rule: the file must equal exactly what `recording_text` writes from what the loader parsed (sorted keys, indent 2, ASCII, LF only, one final newline), which covers all of those in one check, and a probe showed that writing, loading and writing again returns identical bytes for text holding Chinese characters, curly quotes, an emoji, a backslash and a carriage return and newline inside the text. It also recomputes the fingerprint from the stored request, refuses another fingerprint format, refuses a response that breaks a promise the meter checks (prompt tokens over the bound, output over the limit, another served model), so a replay can never close a meter by accident, checks the file name (the purpose, with a colon written as an underscore because Windows forbids it, a dash and the first twelve characters of the fingerprint), refuses a stray file in a scenario folder and two recordings of one request, and keeps hand and live recordings apart by their request ids (a hand recording's is absent or starts with `hand-`, a live one's is a provider id).
- The fake client (`llm/fake.py`) takes loaded recordings, does no I/O, reads no environment variable and prints nothing. `input_token_bound` returns the recorded bound and `complete` the recorded response object; a miss in either is `RecordingMissing` carrying the fingerprint and the purpose (the message shows twelve characters of the fingerprint and the purpose), so a miss in the bound call stops the meter before an attempt. `calls` is the ordered log of answered calls and `unused()` the sorted fingerprints no call asked for in either method, so a scenario test can prove that its folder holds no stale recording. The constructor refuses two recordings with one fingerprint. There is never a default answer.
- Layout and tests of the fixtures. Recordings live in `tests/fixtures/llm/<scenario>/` with a manifest of sha256 values, a source, a time, the fingerprint, the model and the purpose and no url; D29's `-text` attribute already covers the folder. `tests/test_fixture_manifests.py` keeps its checks on the EDGAR and Nasdaq folders exactly as they were, including the url and fetch time check, and the scenario folders join the byte checks (hash equals bytes, the manifest and the folder list each other, the manifest is LF only, the attribute is set) and get their own check (no url, the exact field set, the entry equal to what the writer derives from the file). The scenario folders are found by listing the directory, so a test asserts that the list is not empty and holds `hand`; a deleted or misnamed folder would otherwise leave the checks passing over nothing. A size ceiling: a test sums the bytes of every file under `tests/fixtures/llm/` and fails above 10 MiB (10,485,760 bytes); like the tool size ceilings it moves only by a recorded decision.
- The hand recording is one file, `tests/fixtures/llm/hand/hand.format_proof-168b127f9b5e.json` (1,484 bytes, 41 lines, sha256 `a9c1c99b79dd1d3f5ea21d16461f8951ac76104e5db750592e9991a8b5b74ada`, fingerprint `168b127f9b5ed81ebced46c7e26f609054ef7c40dad9ec4e3d8f49f6dcdc370c`), marked `hand` in the file and the manifest, with the request id `hand-recording-1`, recorded 2026-11-16T12:00:00+00:00. Its purpose is `hand.format_proof` and its format name `format_proof`, labels chosen so that it reads as a proof of the format and not as a reader contract, which the reader's plan pass has not decided; neither label is in the fingerprint. The fingerprint was recomputed with plain `hashlib` from D57's canonical form, independently of this repository's code, by the architect, and by a test from a hand-typed canonical text. Replayed through the meter with the shipped price table it costs 0.0000223 (88 input tokens at 0.10, 40 five-minute cache write tokens at 0.125 and 17 output tokens at 0.50, per million) against a worst case of 0.00013 for its bound of 150 and its limit of 200, and a token cap of 350 allows the call where 349 stops it, as a dollar cap of 0.00013 allows it and 0.000129 stops it. The gated live script that makes `live` recordings, and the key setting, come with the adapter; its gate is `LISTINGLENS_ALLOW_LIVE=1` (D53), beside the existing `LISTINGLENS_ALLOW_NETWORK=1` of the scripts that read the SEC, and it only has to call `make_recording`, `write_recording` and `write_manifest`.
- Evidence: the fixture conventions read in `tests/test_fixture_manifests.py` and `scripts/record_fixtures.py`; the round-trip probe; the CI workflow; the hand recording built by the writer itself and checked as above; 59 scripted mutations, each restored byte for byte (hashes compared) and each caught by a named test: every hook and cap of the loader, each consistency rule and each promise, the canonical-form check and each way a writer could differ (key order, ASCII, indent, the final newline, a text mode that translates newlines, a colon left in a name, the hash taken over other bytes, a manifest merge that drops entries or is not sorted), each fake behavior (a default answer, a miss in each method not raised, the purpose in the key, the call log, `unused()`, duplicates), the error family and messages, the `llm/` guards covering both new modules, the secret scan, the discovery of the scenario folders returning nothing, the size ceiling lowered below the hand folder, and the shipped file and its manifest changing by one character.
- Options considered: the request as D57's canonical text (cannot rebuild a request); a recording holding an ordered list of answers or an error (rejected: the stub does that); one folder for all recordings (rejected: a memo run is the unit that is re-recorded together); separate ASCII and LF checks (rejected: the canonical-form rule covers both and more); the writer in the live script only (rejected: it must match the loader's form exactly and be tested without a model); a `Z` suffix for the time (rejected: one spelling round-trips); the hash check of the manifests skipped for scenario folders (rejected).
- Why: a replay is only as good as the recording, so a recording says what it was made from, proves its own key, keeps the promises the meter checks, and cannot be mistaken for a live answer or carry a secret, and any byte that changes shows in a hash.
- Revisit if: a reading call needs a section larger than the size cap, the recordings pass the 10 MiB ceiling (then recordings should refer to the reader's text fixtures instead of copying the section text), Phase 5 needs several answers per request (a judge's repeats), or recordings need to carry a thinking summary.

### D59: The run meter and the budget in llm/ (third of four llm commits; D58 is the second)
- Date: 2026-11-16
- Decision: `llm/budget.py` holds `Budget` and `Meter`, and `llm/ledger.py` holds the records. `Budget` has three required caps and no defaults: attempts, tokens (prompt plus output) and dollars (an exact `Decimal`; a float, a zero and a boolean are refused). The values are chosen where a run is built, from measured costs. `Meter(client, prices, budget)` is itself a `ModelClient` that wraps another one, so the reader and the memo writer cannot tell it from the client it wraps; it takes an explicit `PriceTable`, does no I/O and runs on the caller's thread (D42). Its `input_token_bound` delegates to the wrapped client and changes nothing.
- Before each call, in this order: a closed meter raises its error again (below); the model must have a price entry (`UnknownModel`, before the client is asked for anything); the client is asked for its input bound once; the bound must be a positive `int` (`type(bound) is int`, so a boolean or a float is refused; anything else is `BoundBroken`, which is not an attempt and charges nothing); the worst case of D58 is priced; then the three caps are checked in a fixed order, attempts (used plus one), tokens (used plus the bound plus `max_tokens`) and dollars (spent plus the worst case). A figure equal to its cap is allowed, one unit over raises `BudgetExceeded` naming the first failing cap and carrying figures only, and the wrapped client's `complete` is never called. A call the budget stops, and a call whose bound could not be obtained, is not an attempt and charges nothing; neither closes the meter.
- A call that is sent holds its worst case and its tokens (the bound plus `max_tokens`) from the moment it starts. If the wrapped client raises, the attempt counts, the reservation stays charged because what was billed is unknown, the call is recorded as failed with no usage and no cost, and the very same exception object is re-raised (no wrapping). If it returns, the actual usage is priced at the requested model's entry (D58), the reservation is replaced by the actual tokens and cost, and the record is written first. Only then are the promises checked: the served model must equal the requested id (`ServedModelMismatch`; the table has no snapshots, so a different id is an error), actual prompt tokens must be at or under the bound and actual output at or under `max_tokens` (`BoundBroken`). There is no check of the actual cost against the worst case, because with the loader's rule that rates never fall in a larger band and the highest input-side rate in the worst case it follows from the two token checks and could never fire; D58's sweep of 1,350 cases covers it instead.
- A broken promise closes the meter. The caps rest on the wrapped client's promises, and a caller that catches the error (a reading tool may turn an error into a not-available result and carry on) must not keep spending against a cap that no longer holds. After `BoundBroken` or `ServedModelMismatch`, including an invalid bound, every later `complete` raises a new error of the same class with the original figures, before the model is looked up or the bound is asked for, with no attempt counted and nothing charged. A transport failure does not close the meter.
- The ledger. `Meter.records` is a tuple of `CallRecord`, and `Meter.totals()` is a `RunTotals` (attempts, tokens, dollars, failed calls and the version of the price table). A record holds the purpose, the requested model, the served model or none, the request fingerprint (D57), the outcome (completed, truncated, refused, context_full or failed), the refusal category or none, the usage or none, the cost lines or none, the dollars and tokens charged, the worst case, the input bound and the request id or none. It holds no prompt, system text, schema or response text; a test pins the exact field set, bans field names that could hold text, and plants a marker in the system text, a message and the response text and finds it in no record, total or error message. The refusal category is a slug validated in D57, kept so that the ledger shows which refusals may have been unbilled and so over-counted (D58). A validator ties the shape together: a failed record has no response fields, an answered one has them all, and only a refusal has a category. Every error message of this commit carries figures and model ids only: a bound that is not an integer is named by its type and never shown, and a closed meter keeps the figures of the broken promise, never the request or the response.
- Evidence: hand-worked oracles on Sonnet 5.5 for a request with a bound of 10,000 and a limit of 4,000 (worst case 0.08, 14,000 tokens): each cap at exactly the cap and one unit over, what was spent or used counting against the next call, the cap order, a stopped call charging nothing, the actual usage replacing the reservation (0.031 and 9,500 tokens), a failed call holding 0.08 and 14,000 and stopping the next call under a cap of 0.11, the same exception object coming out, a broken bound on input and on output, a served-model mismatch priced at the requested entry, the unknown model before any bound call, a bool, a zero, a negative and a float bound, every later call refused once closed with the scripted client's call log showing no further call, a refusal category reaching the record, and the totals summing exactly (0.0554). The scripted client in `tests/llm_stub.py` plays answers or exceptions in order and logs each call; it is not the replaying fake of the next commit. 58 scripted mutations, each restored byte for byte (hashes compared) and each caught by a named test: the check order and placement, each cap with its equal and off-by-one cases, the reservation, each way a failed call could be lost or replaced, each promise unchecked or exclusive, each place a broken promise could fail to close the meter, a transport failure closing it, the closed meter asking for the bound or raising another class, the record fields, the refusal category, the totals, the budget's caps gaining defaults or taking a float, and the `llm/` guards covering both new modules.
- Options considered: a post-call cost check (rejected above); closing only on a broken bound (rejected: a served model that differs breaks the price the cap was computed from); a fresh exception object versus the stored one when closed (a new object of the same class and figures, so repeated raising does not pile traceback on one instance); recording a budget-stopped call (rejected: it was never sent, and a ledger of sent calls is what a cost report needs); wrapping the client's exception in a typed one (rejected: the caller and the adapter's own typed errors must see the original); a default budget (rejected: the caps come from measured costs at the workflow commit).
- Why: the dollar cap is only as good as the promises of the client under it, so the meter checks them, records the truth first, refuses to spend on a cap it can no longer trust, and never lets a lost call go uncharged.
- Revisit if: the adapter's plan pass finds a promise it cannot keep (a bound it cannot give), a second provider needs a different stop vocabulary, a reading tool needs to resume after a broken promise (a new meter, not a reset), or the workflow commit's measured costs show the worst case is too loose to be useful.

### D58: The price table and the exact cost of a call in llm/ (second of four llm commits; D57 is the first)
- Date: 2026-11-15
- Decision: the prices live in `llm/price_table.json`, shipped as package data like the rulesets and read with `importlib.resources`, so they load from a source tree and from an installed wheel alike (checked on a wheel built from a scratch copy of the source: the file ships and reads from inside the archive). The file holds a format name, a table version, the currency (USD), the unit (per million tokens) and one entry per model. Each entry carries its own source page, the date it was checked and who checked it (Austin Smith, as the rulesets' `verified_by` does), and an ordered list of bands, each labeled with the pricing page's own row text. A band's ceiling is on the whole prompt, meaning uncached input plus both cache writes plus cache reads (`Usage.prompt_tokens`); the first band whose ceiling is at or above the prompt applies, and the last band is open. On the pricing page read on 2026-11-15, equal to the figures of D53 read on 2026-11-09, Haiku 5.5 is priced "for prompts up to 100,000 tokens" and "for prompts over 100,000 tokens", so a prompt of exactly 100,000 is the low band and 100,001 the high one, and Sonnet 5.5 has one band because it includes the full context window at its standard price. Prices are strings of one to six decimal places read into exact `Decimal`. The loader refuses a float or an exponent anywhere in the file (the JSON hooks refuse every number with a decimal point, and `NaN` and `Infinity`), an integer or negative or signed price, a price of zero (a zero has no counterpart on the page and would silently undercount a line), a seventh decimal place, a duplicate key, an unknown field at any level, an empty list of bands or models, a duplicate model, unordered or equal ceilings, an open band that is not last, a last band that has a ceiling, a rate that is lower than the same rate in the previous band, a wrong format, currency or unit, a URL that is not https, a bad date, a blank label, a file over 64 KB, a file nested too deeply for the JSON parser and an unreadable file, each as `PriceTableInvalid` naming the file, the record and the field. A strict Pydantic `Decimal` accepts only a `Decimal` instance in Python mode but accepts a JSON number in JSON mode, so the table is validated from parsed data through a before-validator that takes a string only; JSON was chosen over YAML because it is in the standard library and the hooks give the strictness, whereas YAML would need PyYAML inside `llm/` or the strict loader moved out of `standards/`, and the provenance sits in fields either way.
- The cost of a `Usage` (`price_usage`) is five lines in the band its whole prompt falls in: uncached input, five-minute write, one-hour write, cache read and output, each tokens times the rate divided by a million, computed exactly under a context that traps `Inexact` and `Rounded` (probed: the largest realistic line, a million tokens at 4.00, needs nine digits; a context of 60 digits never rounds a rate of six places, and a long run of the largest lines sums exactly). The figures are never rounded; rounding for display is the memo's concern. Thinking is part of the output tokens and is never added (D56). A refusal or an answer cut off at the output limit is priced from the usage the response reports: the refusal page says token counts appear in `usage` either way and that only the `bio`, `frontier_llm` and `reasoning_extraction` refusals before any output are billed, so a refusal in another category is over-counted here, which errs on the safe side of a cap, and the call record of the next commit keeps the refusal category so the ledger shows which may have been unbilled. That a cut-off answer is billed is inferred from the SDK's type documentation (`output_tokens` is "the inclusive, authoritative total used for billing"), not quoted from a page. `CostLines` stores its total and a validator requires it to equal the sum of the lines. The table has no batch rule (D57), and no multiplier for US-only inference, which is 1.1x on every line including Haiku's higher prices when `inference_geo` is set: the neutral request has no such field and the adapter must never send one.
- The worst case of a request (`worst_case_cost`) takes the band the client's input bound falls in, prices the whole bound at the highest of that band's four input-side rates (input, five-minute write, one-hour write and cache read) and `max_tokens` at that band's output rate. The loader's rule that no rate falls in a larger band is what makes this an upper bound for any prompt at or under the bound, including one that lands in a lower band. There is no shortcut to the plain input rate when a request names no cache lifetime: the caching page says a write needs a `cache_control` marker, but that is a promise the adapter makes and this commit cannot prove, and the saving is a fraction of a cent. A deterministic sweep with no new dependency proves the bound: for each model, bounds of 1,000, 99,999, 100,000, 100,001 and 900,000; for each bound and each of three output limits, prompts at the bound, one under it and a hundredth of it, each placed wholly on each of the four input lines and once split across all four; outputs of zero, half the limit and the limit. That is 675 cases per model, 1,350 in all, and the price of what was used is at or under the worst case in every one.
- Not decided here, owed later: the meter and the budget (the next commit, D59) and the fake client and the recordings (D60). Owed to the adapter's plan pass: never send `inference_geo` (with a canary), confirm on the first live run that the response's `model` equals the requested id (the models page says every current id is its own pinned snapshot, so the table has no snapshots field and the meter will refuse a different served model), and map the "Schema is too complex" 400 that D57 already owes.
- Evidence: the pricing page, the refusal-billing section, the models page and the caching page read on 2026-11-15; a probe of strict `Decimal` fields, of the JSON hooks and of the trapping context (scratch script, nothing imported from `listinglens`); a wheel built from a scratch copy; hand-worked oracles for every line of every band, the band edge from both sides, reads and both writes counting toward the ceiling, a mixed usage, thinking, a refusal, a cut-off answer and zero usage; an independent hand-typed copy of every price, label, date and provenance field that the shipped file must equal exactly, plus the documented cache multiples (1.25 and 2 times input for the two writes, 0.1 times for a read and 0.05 times on Sonnet 5.5); and 76 scripted mutations, each restored byte for byte (hashes compared) and each caught by a named test: the band edge, the whole-prompt band choice, every refusal in the list above, each cost line and its rate, the context, the total, each input-side rate in the worst-case maximum, the shipped prices and their provenance moving, and the `llm/` guards covering both new modules.
- Options considered: YAML (see above); a Python module of literals (rejected: no data diff and no fail-loud loader); provenance for the whole table (rejected: a later non-Anthropic row carries its own page); a `snapshots` field (rejected: no dated id exists for these models); pricing at the served model (rejected: the requested entry is the one the cap was computed from, and a different served model is an error); the plain input rate for the worst case of a request with no cache lifetime (rejected above); a cost check after the call (rejected in the next commit's design: it follows from the two token checks and the loader's rule and could never fire); one commit for the table and the meter (rejected: about 570 lines of source and 900 of tests).
- Why: a wrong price is a wrong cap and a wrong cost figure in a memo, so the data carries its source and its checker and fails loud, no float can enter it, no line can silently cost nothing, and a price changed without a decision fails a test.
- Revisit if: a price or a band changes (a new table version, and a new copy in the tests), a dated model id appears, batch is used (Phase 5), the pages change which refusals are billed, or the table grows rows for another provider.

### D57: The model interface in llm/: a pure request, response and usage, and the error family (first of three commits)
- Date: 2026-11-14
- Decision: `llm/` is a pure package that the reading call, the memo writer, the run meter and every provider adapter go through. It imports only the standard library, `pydantic` and `listinglens.core`; a guard test enforces that allow-list, bans `anthropic`, `httpx`, `httpx2`, `httpcore2`, `mcp`, `mcp_types` and `dotenv` by name, forbids `os.environ`, `os.getenv` and `print`, and checks that nothing outside `llm/` imports `anthropic` (the adapter commit narrows this to the one file that may). The server's "no layer below imports the SDK" tuple gains `llm`. The work is three commits: this one (the interface, usage, the schema subset, the fingerprint, the first errors and the leaf guard), then the price table, cost math and run budget, then the fake client and the recorded-answer format; the adapter follows.
- The protocol has two blocking methods and no streaming, no async and no pool (D42). `complete(request)` returns a response. `input_token_bound(request)` returns an upper bound on the input tokens, not an exact count: the budget needs a bound, and another provider may have no counting endpoint. How the adapter produces it, from a counting endpoint or from a conservative local bound, is decided in the adapter's plan pass with evidence on that endpoint's price and rate limits, since the endpoint adds a request before every call. The fake returns a recorded figure.
- The request (`ModelRequest`) carries a model id, a system text with an optional cache lifetime (five minutes or one hour), a tuple of user and assistant turns, a response format, a positive output limit, an optional effort and a purpose label. It has no field for `temperature`, `top_p`, `top_k`, a prefill, tools or a tool choice, and `extra="forbid"` makes any such field an error; the first and last turns must be user turns and the roles must alternate, which rules out a prefill structurally. A provider's own limits never live here: the SDK's 16,000-token ceiling for a non-streaming request is a transport rule and moves to the adapter, decided together with whether the adapter streams internally behind the blocking interface. The response (`ModelResponse`) carries the text, how it stopped, a refusal category, the usage, the model that served it and the provider's request id. Refusal, truncation at the output limit and a full context window are typed outcomes on the response, so the caller decides (the reader turns them into not-available, the writer into a failed memo); a refused response carries no text at all, so it can never be parsed as an answer, and only a refusal has a category. A missing key, an unknown model, a budget stop and a transport failure are errors.
- The response format is canonical JSON schema text (sorted keys, no spaces, ASCII), so a request is hashable and its fingerprint exact.
