The maxima over six companies are unchanged apart from the `etf` field (about 12 bytes per match): three tiers 77,098 against 80,000 (ATA Creativity; 3.6 percent) and 163,264 on the wire against 180,000; one tier 23,677 against 25,000 (Credit Suisse; 5.3 percent) and 50,050 against 52,000 (3.8 percent); `extract_financials` 7,381 and 15,934 against 8,000 and 17,000. No ceiling moved.
- Smoke: `tier_row` maps `directory_funds_only` to 11; Credit Suisse's expected row is 11 and a hard check; `check_problems` gains an agreement rule (every matched row flagged means row 11 and no tier, row 11 means exactly that, and no tier chosen from the directory names a flagged ticker); the ETF flag is no longer a finding, and a name that reads as a note or fund on an unflagged row is an informational L6 finding. Live read 2026-11-06 through the real server: Credit Suisse, continued, no tier named: `directory_funds_only`, row 11, no tier, three matches with `etf` true and the ETRACS names in `nasdaq_status`; with `tier=global`, `named` and one tier assessed; the published tool description carries both new sentences; the contact appeared 0 times.
- Options considered: the ETF flag plus a name classifier (rejected, above); the flag only with the name shown (adopted: the name was already cited in `nasdaq_status`); renumbering the rows so the new one sits at 6 (rejected: D46 to D49 and the smoke cite the numbers); a rulebook citation in the note (rejected: the search found filings from several years, not the current rulebook, and the standards are Nasdaq's); a name on `DirectoryCitationView` and the domain locator for the flag (rejected: the file hash and the symbol already identify the row).
- Why: assessing a fund or a note against the operating-company standards answers a question that does not exist for it, and a cited directory match does not make that defensible. The structured flag is the only signal the directory gives, so it decides and nothing else does.
- Revisit if: Nasdaq documents the ETF column for `nasdaqlisted.txt` or changes its meaning, a real issuer mixes flagged and unflagged rows, the directory gains a structured security type (then L6 can be fixed), or L7's eager overflow read becomes a cost. Housekeeping: `scripts/record_fixtures.py` is at 289 lines, so the next recording splits it first. Next: F-live-2 triage, then Phase 3 closes.

### D49: Never measure a figure from an earlier fiscal year as the latest (F-live-1)
- Date: 2026-11-05
- Decision: the engine now refuses to measure a figure that is not from the company's latest fiscal year. Extraction records that year as `FinancialSnapshot.latest_fiscal_year` (`FiscalYearAnchor`: end, accession, form; `extraction/anchor.py`, commit 6cae8e7): the newest end of a full-year USD income or revenue period among the 13 tags derived from `ALIASES` (the candidates of the four monetary flow concepts plus every tag the D35 fallback reads), counting only facts in the concept's expected unit, passing the same full-year test the figures use, and ending on or before their filing date; cited to the fact at that end that `latest_filed` picks (the shared order D31 uses: filed date, then accession); null when no fact qualifies, and never computed from today's date. `Inputs.from_snapshot` carries it, and `margin/windows.py` `stale_problem` (called from `resolve._concept` after the existing kind and label checks, single-period windows only, flows and balances alike) makes a concept figure that ends more than `STALE_AFTER_DAYS = 183` days before the anchor not evaluable. Untouched: a figure ending on or after the anchor or within 183 days before it, every figure when there is no anchor, multi-year windows, derived metrics and external inputs, and `public_float` and `shares_outstanding`, exempt through `COVER_PAGE_CONCEPTS` in `extraction/aliases.py` because the float is dated at the end of the second fiscal quarter and the share count is the cover page's (measured lag of the floats of Trans American, Silvergate and Citigroup: 184 days). The reason is a new kind, `NotEvaluableKind.STALE_FIGURE` (`stale_figure`, last in `REASON_ORDER`), with a detail that names both period ends and both accessions, and the tier caveat gains `stale_concepts` (the concepts whose only figure on hand is from an earlier year; carried through `Caveat`'s order and emptiness checks, `findings`, `CaveatView` and the mapping tables). `extract_financials` shows the anchor as `latest_fiscal_year` beside the figures, and the stale figure itself still appears there, correctly.
- Causes, corrected and confirmed: cause 1 is not what D48 assumed. D35's fallback already competes with the explicit tag on freshness (`select_figure` appends its candidates to the usable list and keeps the latest period); it produced nothing fresher for ATA Creativity (CIK 1420529) because no FY2022 to FY2025 candidate survives its own rules. Per year, in USD, pre-tax minus tax against the two net income tags: 2021 -5,713,543 against `NetIncomeLoss` -5,280,357 and `ProfitLoss` -5,713,543; 2022 -7,045,501 against -6,943,819 and -7,045,501; 2023 -4,748,031 against -4,740,947 and -4,748,031; 2024 -4,945,387 against -4,945,374 and -4,945,387; 2025 -6,870,703 against -6,870,699 and -6,870,703. The total (`ProfitLoss`) satisfies the identity every year but has no witness (no full-year NCI income fact and no USD common-stockholder fact for those years, D35 refuses it), and the parent (`NetIncomeLoss`) fails the exact identity by 433,186, 101,682, 7,084, 13 and 4. The stale primary (`IncomeLossFromContinuingOperations`, 2021-01-01 to 2021-12-31, -5,280,357, 20-F 0001564590-22-015529) therefore won as the best of what survives. Cause 2 is confirmed: nothing compared a figure's period with the rest of the company's.
- The tolerance: a fiscal year is about 365 days and a half year about 182, so the previous year's figure is at least about 350 days behind (ATA Creativity's is 1,461) and a current one is never behind at all. Measured over nine companies, every non-cover-page figure had a lag of 0 or less except ATA Creativity's income figure; Trans American's balances at 2025-09-30 against its anchor of 2024-12-31 are 273 days newer, which is not stale. Any value from about 30 to 340 days separates them; 183 leaves room for 52 and 53 week years and odd year ends.
- Why not `window_mismatch`: it means the figure does not fit the rule's period (a flow for a balance, a quarter-end balance for a fiscal-year window), and the engine files every single-period `window_mismatch` under `needs_year_end_balance`, which tells a reader to look for a year-end balance that exists. A stale figure fits the window and is the wrong year, so it gets its own kind, its own caveat field, and its own reading: nothing is missing, the filer has not reported the figure for the latest year.
- What changed, on the same bytes as before (the recorded companies' dump at 3ec3931 against this commit): ATA Creativity only. `nasdaq-5550b3-c-net-income-latest` (Capital, continued, all five pathways) and `nasdaq-5505b3-c-net-income-latest` (Capital, initial, traditional IPO) moved from breached at -5,280,357 to not evaluable with primary reason `stale_figure`; their branch moved from false to unknown and the cell's translated figures from two to one; the Capital initial caveat gained `stale_concepts: [income_from_continuing_operations]`. Every verdict is unchanged (`cannot_determine`: the common requirements need market inputs ListingLens does not have). The continued Capital caveat does not name the stale rule, because it sits in an any-standard part that the clear equity rule (4,582,681) already decides; the initial Capital cell has no decided standard, so its caveat does. That is 6 of ATA Creativity's 30 cells; every other cell of every other recorded company differs only by the new empty `stale_concepts`. The check_standards results of Trans American, Luckin, Xiao-I and GE are otherwise identical.
- Known limitation: ATA Creativity's FY2025 income from continuing operations stays a gap. The nine-month 2025 noncontrolling-interest fact (`NetIncomeLossAttributableToNoncontrollingInterest`, USD, 2025-01-01 to 2025-09-30, -4, 6-K 0001104659-25-113200) matches the 4 dollar gap between the parent and the total FY2025 net income, which supports reading the gap as untagged noncontrolling interest, but D35 requires a full-year witness and none exists for 2025, so the figure correctly stays a gap.
- Recorded: Xiao-I's anchor cites its 20-F/A (0001213900-26-060270, filed 2026-05-22) rather than the original 20-F (0001213900-26-057986, filed 2026-05-15) through the shared order, so the F-live-2 fix, which changes that order, will reach the anchor with no second change; a test pins both.
- Size ceilings (regression guards, not budgets, D46): ATA Creativity is now one of five recorded companies in the size tests. After this commit the largest results are `resolve_company` 970 bytes on the wire, `list_filings` 12,986 (20) and 59,718 (100), `extract_financials` 15,934 (7,381 as model JSON), `check_standards` 49,692 for one tier (23,400) and 163,238 for three (77,086). Only `extract_financials` crossed an existing ceiling, so its wire ceiling went from 15,000 to 17,000; the others stand (52,000, 180,000, 64,000, 14,000 and 1,100 on the wire; 25 KB and 80 KB as model JSON, where the three-tier result is within 3.6 percent of its ceiling and is the one to watch). The smoke's copy of the ceilings follows. The company was not excluded.
- Smoke: the F-live-1 detector now checks what the fix guarantees, that no `check_standards` finding rests on a figure ending more than 183 days before `extract_financials`' `latest_fiscal_year`; the stale figure is printed as shown beside the anchor and is not a finding. Live read, 2026-11-05, through the real server: ATA Creativity's anchor is 2025-12-31 (20-F 0001104659-26-037267), `income_from_continuing_operations` is still shown at -5,280,357 for 2021-12-31, and `nasdaq-5550b3-c-net-income-latest` is `stale_figure` with the detail above instead of breached; equity is clear at 4,582,681 and the verdict is `cannot_determine`; the contact appeared 0 times.
- Options considered: reusing `window_mismatch` (rejected, above); demoting the stale figure to a gap in extraction (rejected: the guard then lives only where a snapshot is built, and the figure is worth showing with its period); loosening D35's identity to a tolerance or admitting the parent figure without a witness (rejected in D35: absent facts prove nothing); the anchor from `submissions` `reportDate` (rejected: a fetch and a profile field for a fact the facts already hold); a fixed 400 day threshold against the newest figure (rejected: it compares figures with each other, not with the company's year).
- Why: a figure from 2021 measured against a 2025 rule and reported as breached is a false statement with a citation on it; refusing to measure it, saying why, and keeping it visible is the honest answer.
- Revisit if: a filer's scanned flow tags all go stale together, so the anchor itself lags and a stale figure passes (the R1 condition); a real filer needs a tolerance other than 183 days; a third concept is dated by its own calendar; or F-live-2's fix changes which accession the anchor cites. Next: L3 (structured-product issuers assessed as operating companies), which blocks Phase 3, then F-live-2.

### D48: The live smoke of the MCP server, what it found, and the README
- Date: 2026-11-05
- Decision: `scripts/smoke_mcp.py` (with `smoke_mcp_flows.py`, `smoke_mcp_run.py` and the pure `smoke_mcp_checks.py`, split to stay under the line cap) is a manual live smoke of the real server, gated exactly like `smoke_edgar.py` and the recorder: it refuses unless `LISTINGLENS_ALLOW_NETWORK=1`, sits outside `testpaths`, and CI never runs it. It starts the server as a child process (`python -m listinglens.server`) and drives it over stdio with the SDK's client, with a read timeout of 120 seconds, so the environment, the entry point, the lifespan and the child's stderr are all the real ones. It checks plausibility and never exact values: the company, the tickers, a date of today, newest-first filings that add up, a form filter that is exact, every figure a decimal string with a dashed accession, the tier-table row a result sits in, a directory stamp and hash of the right shape, every finding's measured value equal to the same concept in `extract_financials`. Each call's wire size is judged against the D47 ceilings, and an overrun is a finding, not a failure, because those ceilings came from four recorded companies. The script makes 58 measured tool calls (plus 4 error probes) to 12 companies, 13.2 seconds of call time in all, and fixes nothing.
- The SEC contact: the script reads it with `Settings()` (the environment or `.env`; the server itself still never reads `.env`) and gives it to the child only through its environment block, with an absolute temporary cache directory and the log level, so it is on no command line. It is never printed, logged or written: every line goes through a redaction, the raw text of every line is counted for the contact before it is redacted, the child's stderr goes to a file the script owns and is scanned, and the run fails if the contact appears anywhere. The cache is a fresh temporary directory removed at exit, so every run is cold and nothing is left in the repository. Result of the live run: the contact appeared 0 times in the script's output and in the server's 38 stderr lines.
- Live run, 2026-11-05 (the Nasdaq directory file was 0 days old, stamp `1105202608:31`, 5,610 rows; the server started in 3.05 seconds). It ran twice: the first run found three defects in the script itself, not in the server, which were fixed and the run repeated. The SDK's `send_ping` is removed as of 2026-07-28 and only works in legacy mode, so the stall probe now sends a `tools/list` request that skips the client's cache; the staleness detector had flagged `public_float` (measured at the end of the second quarter, and dated apart from the fiscal figures, like the cover-page share count) for the 2025 and 2026 filers, so those two concepts are excluded from it; and Trans American's expected ticker and tier row became findings because the SEC no longer lists a ticker for it. The second run: 142 checks passed, 0 failed, 17 findings. Slowest call 1.63 seconds (GE `extract_financials`, cold), most calls 0.05 to 0.6 seconds, no read timeout fired. The largest wire sizes against their ceilings: `resolve_company` 2,360 bytes for BMO (ceiling 1,100); `list_filings` at the cap of 100 58,578 (64,000); `extract_financials` 15,722 for ATA Creativity (15,000); `check_standards` for one tier or none 49,700 for Credit Suisse AG (52,000), and for an initial listing with three tiers 154,142 for Xiao-I (180,000). Only those three call kinds exceed a ceiling, and only because a live issuer is bigger than the four recorded ones.
- Large filer: Citigroup's companyfacts is 9,019,678 bytes decoded (8.60 MiB, 593 KB compressed) and was the largest of 24 documents measured; fetched directly it took 0.44 seconds and through the server `extract_financials` took 0.62 seconds (9 figures, no gaps). The response cap is 50 MiB of decoded bytes, so the largest real document is 5.8 times below it. No live filer approaches the cap, so the smoke can report the headroom but cannot exercise the refusal; the transport test with a patched cap remains the only proof of it.
- The event loop: while a call runs, the server answers nothing else (D42, D47). A `tools/list` request sent 50 milliseconds into a call was answered only after the call ended: 0.57 seconds into Citigroup's 0.62 second `extract_financials`, 0.07 seconds into Xiao-I's initial three-tier check (0.13 seconds) and 0.29 seconds into a repeated GE check (0.35 seconds). So the wait a host would see is the length of the call it is waiting on, at most 1.63 seconds in this run, with no timeout. What this does not show is a host: the smoke's client is the SDK's, with a 120 second timeout. Whether Claude Desktop or Claude Code cancels, times out or delays a second request is the open question for the manual step after this commit, together with which payload (the text copy, the structured content, or both) the host gives the model.
- Findings, none fixed here. F-live-1 (reproduced): ATA Creativity's `income_from_continuing_operations` is -5,280,357 for the period ending 2021-12-31 (20-F accession 0001564590-22-015529) while the other seven figures end 2025-12-31 (accession 0001104659-26-037267); a defect, and Phase 3 does not close until it is fixed. F-live-2 (reproduced): all seven of AGM Group's figures cite form `POS AM` with no fiscal period, and its share count and public float are gaps; D31's deferred prefer-annual tie-break, triaged after F-live-1. L3 (new, blocking, same status as F-live-1): when a company's only Nasdaq listings are exchange-traded notes or funds, the operating-company continued-listing standards do not apply to that issuer, and assessing it against them answers a question that does not exist for it; a cited directory match does not make that defensible. Evidence: Credit Suisse AG (CIK 1053092) has three tickers, `GLDI` with security name `ETRACS Gold Shares Covered Call ETNs due February 2, 2033`, `SLVO` with `ETRACS Silver Shares Covered Call ETNs due April 21, 2033`, and `USOI` with `ETRACS Crude Oil Shares Covered Call ETNs due April 24, 2037`, each in market category `G`, financial status `N`, ETF flag `Y`, not a test issue; `check_standards` for a continued listing with no tier named returned row 6, tier Global, verdict `cannot_determine`, and its `extract_financials` returned one figure and eight gaps. It belongs to tier selection and gets its own plan pass after the F-live-1 fix; the smoke now prints the security name and the ETF flag of every matched directory row for every company so that pass has the evidence. L4: the D47 ceilings are recorded-fixture ceilings (above); and `resolve_company` carries `tickers` and `exchanges` as two parallel lists full of repeats (BMO: 45 tickers, 45 exchange strings, 2 distinct), so a candidate improvement after Phase 3 is to publish ticker and exchange pairs; nothing changes now. L1: companies that report under IFRS or file 40-F (Royal Bank of Canada and BMO on 40-F, Credit Suisse AG on a 20-F) have companyfacts but the tools read one figure (the cover-page share count) and eight gaps; reading them is Phase 7 work. New: Trans American no longer has a ticker in the SEC's submissions (`tickers []`), so it now lands in tier-table row 4 and not row 5, which D42 recorded for the recorded fixtures; the fixtures are unaffected. L5: the ticker map has no ambiguous ticker and one ambiguous name, `Wiseqey Corp.`, which the smoke asks for and receives as a readable error listing both CIKs (`0001738699` and `0002107968`).
- Confirmed assumptions: D30's missing-companyfacts case is a 404 (its body is an XML `NoSuchKey` document, which nothing reads), and through the live server SPY (CIK 884394) returns `facts_status` `no_xbrl_facts` with no figures or gaps, while `check_standards` for a named tier ran the engine on the empty bag with no findings and no invented gap reason; Silvergate Capital (CIK 1312109, delisted, no tickers) lands in row 4 with facts available; a continued listing with a tier named is row 3, an initial listing with a tier named is row 2 and an initial listing with none is row 1 (three tiers in order). Not reachable live: the response cap, row 7 (no multi-tier issuer was found among those probed) and rows 9 and 10 (the directory cannot be made to fail on demand); they stay covered by tests.
- README: it describes the finished product, not a status snapshot, so it does not need updating each time a phase lands. The original opening and "What it does" list stay as the description of what ListingLens is built to do; what exists today is recorded in DECISIONS.md and in the tools' own published descriptions, which state the current limits to every model that calls them. The Data line now names Nasdaq's symbol directory beside SEC EDGAR, and the old "Run it yourself" placeholder is replaced by a run-it section: uv 0.13.4 or later, no API key, the three environment variables, a host configuration with an env block (Windows with the installed script, macOS or Linux with the module form), exit status 2 for a bad variable, no host product's configuration path named, a note on .env.example, and the manual smoke command.
- Evidence: 32 offline tests of the script (the gate, the redaction and its raw-text leak count, the child's environment, the ceilings and the wire size against the test suite's, the tier-table row classifier, each plausibility rule against a recorded result and a damaged copy, the two finding detectors, the directory-row check for a fund or a note); 25 scripted mutations (the gate, the redaction, the leak count, the environment, a ceiling, the wire size, the staleness threshold and the float exclusion, the source-form rule, the row classifier, the stamp age, the ETF flag, and each rule) were each caught, with the files restored byte for byte.
- Options considered: exact expected values (rejected: the data moves, as Trans American's ticker did); running the server in process (rejected: it would not test stdio, the environment or the child's stderr); a repository-local cache directory (rejected: warm repeat runs and stray `-wal` files in `git status`); failing the run on a ceiling overrun (rejected: the ceilings were set from four companies); recording the live responses as fixtures (rejected: the point of the smoke is that it is live); reading `.env` in the server (rejected again).
- Why: the unit tests prove the server against recorded data, and only a live run shows what real issuers do to it; reporting each surprise with its evidence, and fixing none in the same commit, keeps each later fix reviewable on its own.
- Revisit if: a real host stalls or times out on a call, the live data moves a tier-table row for a company the smoke treats as stable, F-live-1's fix changes the figures a latest-year rule measures, or L3's plan pass changes what a directory match means. Housekeeping: `scripts/record_fixtures.py` (300 lines) and `core/errors.py` (295) are untouched.

### D47: The MCP stdio adapter: a thin server over the in-process service, and what the SDK does and does not do
- Date: 2026-11-01
- Decision: `server/` is the only package that imports the SDK (under both of its import names, `mcp` and `mcp_types`) and holds the stdio adapter over D46's `ToolService`: four `async def` tools named `resolve_company`, `list_filings`, `extract_financials` and `check_standards`, published with the server instructions and tool descriptions approved in D42 (word for word, plain ASCII apostrophes, compared in tests with an independent copy), and annotated read-only, idempotent and open-world. `build_server(open_service)` is the only way to build it: the lifespan enters the context manager `open_service()` on the event-loop thread, keeps the service in a small holder the tools read (the SDK's `Context` parameter is typed with an explicit `Any`, which this repo bans), and leaves it on shutdown. Production passes `open_tool_service` over the settings; tests pass the recorded stack or a stub. It is an argument and nothing else, and an AST guard pins that nothing under `server/` reads `os.environ`, `os.getenv` or a dotenv file (pydantic-settings reading the environment inside `ToolSettings` is the one sanctioned reader), so no process environment can choose what is served. `python -m listinglens.server` and the console script `listinglens-server` both run `startup.main`.
- Parameters: each tool's parameters are the argument models' own annotated aliases (`CikArg`, `FormsArg` and the rest, defined once in `tools/args.py` with their descriptions in `tools/arg_text.py`), as keyword-only parameters, and the first line of each tool builds the C3c model. So the SDK validates exactly what the models validate and publishes the models' constraints and descriptions, and a parity test fails the moment a published input schema differs from its model's. A model-typed parameter would have been nested under its parameter name (`{"args": {...}}`, with the model as a `$ref`), which the SDK does for any Pydantic parameter; the flat form is the natural schema for a model to fill and keeps the three selector tools free of `$ref`. SDK limitation, recorded: the SDK's argument model does not forbid extra keys and offers no way to, so a stray top-level key (`as_of`, `price`, a misspelled field) is dropped before our code runs, and nesting under `args` would not close that gap because a stray key beside `args` would be dropped too. The models still refuse extras, and a test pins that a stray key is ignored while the result's own echoes (`evaluated_on`, `requested_tier`, `tier_source`, and the still-missing market inputs in the caveat) show what was actually used; a wrong key such as `company` reaches the readable request for exactly one of cik, ticker or name. Revisit if the SDK gains a way to forbid extra keys. The argument descriptions were rewritten to say what actually happens (for `tier`: all three tiers for an initial listing, the tier Nasdaq's directory gives for the ticker for a continued one, nothing assessed without a ticker or a match, a failure with the reason if the directory cannot be read, and a named tier always assessed).
- Results: each tool returns `Annotated[CallToolResult, Result]`, a result built by hand with the same data twice: the structured content, and compact JSON as the text copy that the spec expects beside a published output schema. The SDK's own text copy is `indent=2` JSON, which on the recorded companies was 2.3 to 2.8 times the structured size on the wire against 2.1 for compact text. The structured content is the Python-mode dump on purpose: with a `CallToolResult` the SDK validates `structured_content` against the output model in Python mode, and these models are strict, so a JSON-mode dict (ISO date strings, arrays) is refused as an internal failure; the Python-mode dict validates, and the SDK serializes it to the JSON the client receives. The output schema is the model's own schema (not wrapped in `result`), generated by the SDK's strict generator, which raises on any warning and does not for any of the four models; every description lands in it. A test pins that the text parses to JSON equal to the structured content, that the structured content validates as JSON against the model, and that an error is always raised as a `ToolError` and never returned as a result.
- Wire size (content text plus structured content plus `isError`, compact JSON-RPC encoding, the largest over Trans American, Luckin, GE and Xiao-I, with the pinned ceiling): `resolve_company` 970 (1,100), `list_filings` at the default of 20 12,884 (14,000) and at the cap of 100 59,718 (64,000), `extract_financials` 13,666 (15,000), `check_standards` for one continued tier 47,818 (52,000) and for an initial listing with all three tiers 162,912 (180,000). The SDK's indented text would have been 1,071, 14,479, 67,313, 17,426, 63,657 and 214,599, each over its ceiling. Hosts put the text block in front of the model and use the structured content for validation or display, so the text copy is the model-facing cost (about 77 KB compact for the three-tier answer); a host that forwards both pays the wire figure. The ceilings are regression guards, not budgets, and trimming the views still waits for Phase 4 to measure token cost (D46).
- Errors: a context manager inside each tool turns the readable failures into `ToolError`, whose message the SDK sends as `Error executing tool <name>: <message>` with `isError` true and no structured content: `ToolArgumentError`, `AmbiguousCompany` (up to ten `cik (name)` candidates, then a count of the rest, labelled as data, and a request to retry with a cik), `CompanyNotFound`, and `RateLimited`, `UpstreamError`, `MalformedPayload`, `ResponseTooLarge`, `TooManyRedirects` and `UndecodableDocument`. Every message is built from the error's structured fields, never from `str(exc)`, because a free-form detail can carry text taken from an SEC response; that detail is cut to 200 characters and quoted as data, and the full text goes to the log. Everything else is left to the SDK, which tells the caller only `Error executing tool <name>` and logs the traceback at ERROR: the bare `InputValidationError` an engine model raises on a broken invariant (a forged rule set proves it arrives masked, with the bare class found under the SDK's wrapper in the log), `UnrepresentableNumber`, `StandardsError`, `MarginError`, `AllowlistViolation`, `MissingUserAgent` mid-run, and anything else. Arguments the SDK refuses before our code runs (a limit of 101, an unknown enum value, an integer CIK) are readable tool errors with the validation text. The SEC contact is not in any error field; a canary contact is searched for in every result, structured content and log line across successes and failures.
- Process model: unchanged from D42 and now proven end to end. The tools are `async def` and call the synchronous service on the loop thread, the lifespan opens and closes it on that same thread (the SDK awaits an `async def` tool directly, sends a plain `def` tool to `anyio.to_thread.run_sync`, and enters the lifespan inside the loop that `anyio.run` starts in the calling thread). A test fires eight concurrent calls across all four tools through the in-process client and records one thread id equal to the lifespan's, with at most one call in flight (each call holds open briefly so overlap would show); a tool made a plain `def`, or a call dispatched through `to_thread`, fails it. The cost is as in D42: a call blocks the loop, including throttle and retry sleeps, so a cancel or ping waits. Revisit on a remote transport, a second concurrent client, or a host stall seen in the live smoke. The dispatch module behind the SDK's request loop was not read; the test, not an assumption, carries the claim.
- Startup and logging: `ServerSettings` adds an optional `LISTINGLENS_LOG_LEVEL` (default WARNING, case and spaces tolerated) to `ToolSettings` and never reads a `.env` file. Any configuration problem (a missing or placeholder contact, a missing, relative or unusable cache directory, a bad level) prints one stderr message that names the problem by variable and never by value, names both required variables and the host's `env` block, says a `.env` file is not used, and exits 2 with nothing on stdout; the cache directory is created and proved writable before the loop starts. `MissingUserAgent`'s own text tells the reader to copy `.env.example`, which is false for the server, so it is never shown. An unexpected crash after startup exits 1 with the traceback on stderr. Logging: stdout is the protocol wire, so nothing under `server/` prints and the root logger gets one stderr handler before the SDK builds the server; `MCPServer` always calls `logging.basicConfig` on the root logger (default level INFO), which does nothing when the root already has a handler, so ours owns the format and level and the SDK's call is a no-op. `rich` is an optional extra of the SDK and is not installed, so the SDK's own handler would have been a plain stderr handler anyway. The SDK's stdio transport also points file descriptor 1 at stderr while serving, a backstop and not a substitute. Two real subprocess tests: a stdio session whose `tools/list` returns exactly the four tools with their output schemas, descriptions and annotations (the child's environment sets the contact, an absolute cache directory and an unroutable proxy, so any accidental fetch fails fast), and the missing-contact, relative-directory, absent-directory and file-as-directory cases, each exiting 2 with empty stdout even with a `.env` file in the working directory that would have rescued them; the console script is run the same way.
- Dependency: `mcp==2.3.0` is pinned exactly and the lock gains 22 packages, nothing existing moved (httpx 0.28.1, pydantic 2.13.5, pydantic-settings 2.15.0, pyyaml 6.0.3, certifi 2026.10.19 are unchanged) and `uv audit` reports no known vulnerabilities in 50 packages. They are the SDK itself and `mcp-types`, its HTTP stack (`httpx2`, `httpcore2`, and `httpx2-jsfetch`, installed only on emscripten), its HTTP and OAuth server pieces that this stdio server never starts (starlette, uvicorn, sse-starlette, python-multipart, pyjwt with cryptography and cffi and pycparser), and jsonschema with referencing, rpds-py and attrs, opentelemetry-api, click, truststore, and pywin32 on Windows only. Every package has a wheel for Linux and Windows, so CI on Ubuntu compiles nothing and needs no change. The lock file's own revision field moved from 3 to 5 because the pinned `uv` 0.13.4 wrote it. Nothing in `src` imports `httpx2` or `httpcore2`, and the guard from D45 still enforces it.
- Test infrastructure: the autouse socket guard denied the loopback connection that asyncio's event loop makes on Windows (`socket.socketpair` has no native form there), so every async test failed at loop creation. It now lets a connection through only to a loopback IP literal (`127.0.0.1`, `::1`) and still denies every other address and every name, which is tested; its pieces moved out of `conftest.py` (which had reached the line cap) into `tests/network_support.py`. The server tests run on asyncio only. `core/errors.py` is untouched at 295 lines: C4 added no error class, and the rule stays that the next one splits that module first.
- Evidence: the published surface (identity, instructions, descriptions, annotations, input schemas against their models and against hand-written literals, output schemas) through the in-process client; each tool over the four recorded companies arriving as the service returned it; Xiao-I's hand-worked values through the wire; every error class on every tool through a stub service and the reachable ones through the recorded stack (an unknown company, a 503, a malformed body, the directory down on both sides of the tier table's ninth row); the forged rule set; the canary; the concurrency test; the startup cases in process and in subprocesses; the guards, each with a self-test. 48 scripted mutations (each tool made synchronous or dispatched to a thread, the lifespan opening twice or never closing, a fifth tool, a renamed tool, each annotation, each of the five texts, a signature default drifting from its model, the text copy indented or out of step with the structured content, the structured content in JSON mode, each error row's readability, the contact in a message, a swallowed error, the exit code, the startup message, the preflight, the log level and handler, a dotenv file read, and each boundary guard) were each caught, with the files restored byte for byte.
- Options considered: one model-typed parameter per tool (rejected: `{"args": ...}` with a `$ref`, and it does not forbid stray keys either); the SDK's default text copy (rejected: indented, a third larger for no reader's benefit); a prose summary as the text (rejected: the spec expects the serialized JSON and a text-only host would see no data); text only with no output schema (rejected: no published contract); `str(exc)` as the error message (rejected: SEC-derived text would reach the model); a service seam chosen by an environment variable or a module hook (rejected: reachable from outside); letting a bad cache directory fail inside the lifespan (rejected: a traceback and exit 1 instead of a readable exit 2); reading a `.env` file (rejected: the working directory is the host's choice).
- Why: the adapter should add nothing the service does not say, publish exactly the contract the models enforce, keep an internal fault from looking like the caller's mistake, never let anything but the protocol touch stdout, and fail at startup in words a person configuring a host can act on.
- Revisit if: the SDK gains a way to forbid extra argument keys or to take a model as the whole input; a remote transport, a second concurrent client, or a host stall appears (move the service onto a dedicated owning thread); a host is observed forwarding both payloads to the model (trim the views or drop the structured copy); or the live smoke shows a host that rejects the nested `$defs` in `check_standards`' schema.

### D46: The tool service: four tools as plain methods, the tier table, and what an argument may be
- Date: 2026-10-31
- Decision: `tools/` gains the service that fills D45's models: `ToolService` with `resolve_company`, `list_filings`, `extract_financials` and `check_standards`, built by `open_tool_service(settings)`. No tool takes a price, a market input or a date. Today is the UTC date of the injected clock, the rules applied are the ones in force on that date, and every assessed `TierResult.as_of` equals the result's `evaluated_on` (pinned by test), so the date a reader sees is the date the rules were chosen for. The market conditions (price, market value, holders, market makers) are never supplied, so they stay not measured instead of being answered from a caller's word, and no argument schema contains a field that could carry one (a test walks every schema).
- Arguments: the four argument models are strict, frozen and closed to extra fields. The company is three optional fields, exactly one of which must be given (`cik`, `ticker`, `name`), never one free string classified by its shape, because a name such as NVIDIA is also ticker-shaped. Structural problems (types, lengths, the `limit` range, the shape of `forms`, an unknown enum value) are Pydantic constraints and raise `ValidationError` before the service runs. The one domain-level refusal is `ToolArgumentError` (none or several selectors, a CIK that is not digits, a ticker or name the domain types refuse), raised in `company_ref_of` only. Argument code never raises the base `InputValidationError`: Pydantic lets an exception that is not a `ValueError` propagate unchanged out of a validator, and the server will mask the bare base class as an internal failure, which is what it is when an engine model refuses a broken invariant. A table of 33 refusals asserts each is a `ValidationError` or a `ToolArgumentError` and never the bare class, an AST test pins that `args.py` raises nothing else, and a forged rule set (a standard with no requirements) shows an engine refusal inside `check_standards` leaves as exactly the base class. `list_filings` matches form types whole and without regard to case, so `10-k` matches `10-K` and not `10-K/A` (no prefixes, no wildcards), returns newest first, defaults to 20 and is capped at 100, and its `returned`, `total_matching` and `omitted` always add up.
- The tier table: `check_standards` always tries the directory and keeps a failure instead of raising it, then `select_tiers` (pure, no I/O) applies ten rows. A match is an exact `Symbol` equal to one of the SEC's tickers, a test issue never matches (it is counted in `test_issues_ignored`), and the category selects the tier (Q Global Select, G Global, S Capital). Row 1, initial with no tier named: all three tiers, `how=all_tiers_initial`. Row 2, initial with a tier named: that tier, `named`. Row 3, continued with a tier named: that tier, `named`, the directory result shown beside it so a mismatch is visible. Row 4, continued with none named and no ticker: none, `no_tickers`. Row 5, tickers but no non-test match: none, `no_match`. Row 6, matches in one recognized tier: that tier, `directory`. Row 7, matches in several recognized tiers: each distinct tier in Tier order, labeled by the tickers that selected it, `directory_mixed`. Row 8, a matched row whose category is blank or not recognized: none, `directory_unrecognized_category` (never a subset assessed silently, and an unrecognized status never blocks, it is reported raw). Row 9, continued with none named, at least one SEC ticker and the directory unreadable: the typed ingestion error is raised, because a tier cannot be guessed. Row 10, rows 1 to 4 with the directory unreadable: the assessment proceeds and the directory's state is reported. D50 adds row 11, continued with none named, at least one match and every match flagged ETF=Y: none, `directory_funds_only`; the rows are evaluated in this order: 2 and 3, 1, 4, 9, 5, 11, 8, then 6 and 7, and a flagged row never selects a tier. `how` states only how the tier was selected and never the directory's state, which lives in `directory.available` and `directory.error`; `CheckStandardsResult` echoes `requested_tier` beside `listing_type` and `pathway`. Each row has its own test, including a test issue never matching, mixed categories, an unrecognized category, no tickers, and the directory failing on both sides of row 9.
- No XBRL facts: a company the SEC has no companyfacts for gives `facts_status=no_xbrl_facts`, an empty snapshot (no figures, no gaps) and the engine on that empty bag, so every figure-based rule is not measured with `gap_reason` absent; nothing is invented. It is a result, not an error.
- The profile and the wiring: `CompanyProfile` (tickers, exchanges, entity type, as the SEC spells them) is read through `EdgarRepository.company_profile` and `IngestionService.company_profile` from the same cached submissions the resolution read, so resolve and profile cost one request. `open_cached_client` is extracted from the ingestion wiring so that every repository of the service shares one transport, one throttle bucket, one cache connection and one close; `open_tool_service` hands the directory repository the client's `forget`, so a download that arrives as a 200 but does not parse is evicted instead of served for an hour (proven by an end to end test that fails the first call and succeeds the second). `ToolSettings` never reads a `.env` file (a host can launch the server from any directory), takes the SEC contact from `SEC_EDGAR_CONTACT` through the inherited check, and requires `LISTINGLENS_CACHE_DIR` as an absolute path with no default. The contact goes to the SEC hosts and never to nasdaqtrader.com (a test captures the outgoing headers of a whole `check_standards` call).
- Sizes (serialized as the SDK will send them, UTF-8 bytes, maximum over Trans American, Luckin, GE and Xiao-I): resolve_company 427, list_filings at the default of 20 6,113, at the cap of 100 28,454, extract_financials 6,339, check_standards for one continued tier 22,501, for an initial listing with all three tiers 76,892 (Luckin). Ceilings are pinned at 2 KB, 8 KB, 32 KB, 8 KB, 25 KB and 80 KB. The three-tier ceiling is 80 KB rather than the 75 KB first planned, because the measured result is 1,892 bytes over 75 KB. A ceiling is a regression guard, not a budget, so it was raised rather than trimming the view now; trimming waits until the Phase 4 agent measures what these results cost in tokens.
- Housekeeping: `core/errors.py` is at 295 lines, so the next error added splits that module first.
- Evidence: Xiao-I (AIXI, continued, no tier named, row 6; tier Global from the directory row, status D as of the file stamp `1030202618:01` with its citation; three measured breaches worked by hand) and the tier-source parts of the Luckin, Trans American and GE results, each value re-read from the raw fixture JSON with plain json and none taken from the engine; the ten tier rows each by themselves; the through-the-real-wiring service tests over a routed mock and a fixed clock. 60 scripted mutations (the selector and each refusal's type, form matching, limits and counts, ordering, every tier row, the flow and its date, the empty bag, the profile, the wiring and the settings) were each caught, with the files restored byte for byte.
- Options considered: one free-text company string (rejected: ambiguity between names and tickers); raising the base error from argument code (rejected: masked as internal); failing every call when the directory is down (rejected: rows 1 to 4 do not need it); guessing a tier for a company that is not in the directory (rejected: a named or cited tier only); a separate `directory_unavailable` value of `how` (rejected: two facts, two fields); reading a `.env` in the server (rejected: it depends on the working directory).
- Why: a tool must say what it was asked and what it assumed, refuse a bad argument in words the caller can act on, and keep an internal fault from looking like the caller's mistake.
- Revisit if: a tool needs a caller-supplied input (a price, a date), the directory stops being the source of the tier, or the three-tier result needs to be smaller.

### D45: The projection layer: what a tool shows, as flat views that rearrange the engine and never recompute it
- Date: 2026-10-31
- Decision: `tools/` begins with its pure layer, no I/O and no service: the result and view models, the conversion of domain and engine results into them, and the numbers they carry. `tools/` imports no `mcp` and no `server`. `ResultBase` gives every result the company echo (D9), the date it was evaluated for, and one fixed scope note (figures are as reported in SEC XBRL filings, not verified by ListingLens, a risk signal and not investment advice). `ResolveCompanyResult`, `ListFilingsResult` (with returned, total matching and omitted counts that a validator keeps consistent), `ExtractFinancialsResult` and `CheckStandardsResult` are models only here; the service that fills them is the next chunk.
- Re-model, not wrap: `extract_financials` shows the domain snapshot as flat views with a padded CIK and a dashed accession, because the domain JSON nests `{"cik": {"value": ...}}`, holds bare 18-digit accessions, and its Decimal schema admits a number or a string. Every field of every source model is either mapped to a view field or omitted with a stated reason in one table (35 source models), and a test fails when a field is added to a domain or engine model without an entry, so the projection cannot silently fall behind the domain. The table says where a field goes; whether the value is right is pinned by literal tables.
- Numbers: every Decimal leaves through one function, `decimal_text`, as exact fixed-point text (never `str()`, whose `1E+3` and `1E-7` are not fixed-point numbers), keeping the scale and turning negative zero into zero. The output pattern allows at most 201 integer digits and 300 fraction digits, and the function checks its own output against that same compiled pattern before returning, so the pattern accepts everything the function emits by construction; a value that is not finite, has an exponent beyond 200 in size, or would need more digits than the pattern allows (a value such as `1.` followed by 400 zeros has an exponent of 0 but 400 fraction digits) is refused with `UnrepresentableNumber`, an internal error, instead of failing output validation later as a masked one. The bound on the exponent also keeps a hostile `1E+99999999999` from asking for a 100 GB string. On the recorded data (four companies, every snapshot and all 120 tier cells, 1,938 values emitted) nothing prints with an exponent and the longest string is 103 characters, a 100-digit headroom ratio.
- The findings view: a tier is shown in the order a reader should take it: `reported_financials` (the engine's financial signal, renamed so it cannot read as vouched for), the cited findings (every measured rule with its figure, threshold, comparison, window, headroom, ratio, cushion, figure citation, rule citation, verified flag and translation notes; breached first, then at_risk, then clear, in rule order within each), the common requirements and the standards with their branch states, what could not be measured and why, the translated figures, and last the verdict with its caveat. Rules appear in the tree only as ids; each is detailed once, in findings or in not_measured. The view copies the engine's states, margins, values and verdicts and never recomputes them: the only things it adds are order, grouping, and, for a rule that was not measured, the threshold, comparison, window, unit and what it tests, read from the rule definition. The window (`WindowView`: its kind, and n and m or years where the kind has them) is copied from the rule for measured and unmeasured rules alike: with the threshold and the comparison it is the third part of what a rule tests, and it is what shows why a single figure does not fit, since many single-branch rules sit on a branch named `main` and nothing else would carry it. A validator on the tier view requires every referenced rule to be listed exactly once and nothing else to be listed, and a tier with no rule in force to be empty and say so; an exhaustive test over 120 cells (the four recorded companies, both listing types, three tiers, five pathways) checks the view against the engine with its own state-to-bucket mapping written in the test, field by field, and a second block built with hand-made rule sets checks the ordering.
- The directory views and codes: `NasdaqStatusView` carries a matched ticker's raw category and status, the tier the category selects and the meaning of the status (Nasdaq's wording from its definitions page, read 2026-10-31), the page's URL, a note that it is the exchange's flag and does not feed the assessment, and the citation of the row in the file. A blank or unrecognized code selects no tier and has no meaning, and nothing is guessed. `TierSourceReport.how` states only how the tier was selected (named, all three for an initial listing, no tickers, no match, one tier from the directory, several tiers, or a category not recognized); whether the directory could be read lives in its own field, `directory.available` with `directory.error`, and a validator keeps those consistent. This amends nothing in D42's table, which already kept the directory's state apart from the tier decision; it fixes the wording so that `how` never has to carry both.
- Descriptions: every field whose meaning is not self-evident carries a description that the SDK will publish in the output schema, and the shared meanings (value, thin, headroom, headroom ratio, cushion ratio, reported_financials, rules_in_force) are fixed texts, one definition each, pinned word for word. The fields without a description (an id, a name, a plain figure, a threshold, a nested model that describes itself; 69 of 220) are listed in the test as an exact set, so a new field without a description fails and so does a described field left in the list. The wording was revised in review: the shared `value` text no longer names a cause for unknown; the standards text covers a tier with no standards (a set such as Global Select initial listing rests on its common requirements alone); the verdict text says cannot_determine also covers a selection with no rule in force; and every published description is plain ASCII, with a test that pins it. Every enum the views use (the engine's and the domain's) has its value set pinned as a literal, so a rename in the engine changes what an agent sees only by a decision.
- Boundaries (tests): the lower layers (`core`, `domain`, `ingestion`, `extraction`, `standards`, `margin`) import neither `tools` nor `server`; `tools` imports no `mcp` and no `server`; nothing under `src` imports `httpx2` or `httpcore2`; nothing under `tools/` calls `print`, because once the server exists stdout is the protocol wire. The rule that only the server package may import `mcp` arrives with the server.
- Evidence: the two literal tables of this chunk (Trans American's four cited breaches under the initial Capital rules, and Luckin's three clear findings with their reported-currency translations under the continued Global rules) are worked by hand from the raw fixture JSON, the rules' own text and D41; a test re-reads every measured value, form, fiscal period and accession from the raw JSON with no model, extraction or engine code, and proves every headroom and ratio as an exact fraction. Luckin's implied rates are derived in the test as the exact fraction rounded half even to 28 significant digits, never by calling the engine's own function, and the rounding helper is itself tested at its ties. 42 scripted mutations (the number function and its output check, the pattern, every projection step including the window, the ordering, the completeness validator, field order, descriptions and approved texts, the directory codes, a field added to a domain model, and each boundary guard) were each caught, with the files restored byte for byte.
- Options considered: wrapping the domain snapshot (rejected: nested wrappers, bare accessions, an ambiguous Decimal schema); `str()` for numbers (rejected: exponent forms); trusting the pattern to refuse an over-long value at output validation (rejected: it would surface as a masked error for a legitimate engine value); mirroring the engine's enums in the tool layer (rejected: more code that could drift, where a pin test makes the shared vocabulary a decision); putting the verdict first (rejected: for continued listing it is usually cannot_determine, and a reader leading with it is told nothing); listing rule detail inside the tree (rejected: each rule would appear twice).
- Why: what an agent reads is the product, so every number it sees must be exact, every claim must carry its citation, and the view must be a faithful rearrangement of what the engine measured, with anything unmeasured named beside what it would take to measure it.
- Revisit if: a domain or engine model gains a field (the mapping test fails until it is decided), the engine's vocabulary changes (the pin tests fail), or a tool needs to show a number larger than the output pattern allows.

### D44: One set of shapes for the Nasdaq directory's fields, and a malformed download is evicted from the cache
- Date: 2026-10-31
- Decision: this corrects D43's committed code. D43 left the directory's field rules in several places that could disagree: the category and status pattern (`[A-Z]?`) was written three times (the citation, the row model, the parser), and the parser accepted a Security Name of only whitespace (including a non-breaking, em or ideographic space) that `DirectoryLocator` refused, so `NasdaqDirectory.locator()` could raise on a row the parser had accepted. `NasdaqDirectory.url` and `.sha256` were free strings while the locator required a Nasdaq Trader URL and 64 lowercase hex digits. They agreed only because the repository happened to build both. Now `domain/directory_shapes.py` defines the code pattern, the SHA-256 pattern, the stamp pattern, the URL prefix and `is_blank` (empty or only whitespace in the sense of `str.strip()`) once; the citation, the row model, the directory model and the parser import them, every copy is gone, and a test pins that the pattern text exists in that one module only. The parser now refuses a blank Security Name, the row model and the citation refuse it by the same function, and `NasdaqDirectory` validates its url and hash by the same shapes. This amends D43's parser rules by that one refusal.
- Proof that the parser, the row model and the citation cannot disagree: all 729 combinations of category and status (blank plus A to Z, squared) parse, and every parsed row can be cited; a set of names (a trailing space, a non-breaking space inside, accented and Japanese text, a 300-character name, a zero-width space that `strip()` does not remove) parse and cite, and six blank names (spaces, non-breaking, ideographic and em spaces) are refused by the parser and by the row model; for every ASCII character and several multi-character and non-ASCII strings put in a category or status field, the parser either refuses the file or the row can be built and cited; and every one of the 5,616 rows of the recorded file can be cited. Mutations: loosening the pattern for every user, giving the parser its own looser copy, dropping the parser's blank-name refusal, letting `is_blank` mean empty only, and letting the row model, the citation, the directory url or the directory hash accept what the others refuse are each caught.
- Cache: `CachedEdgarClient.forget(url)` deletes one cached response, and fails open like the rest of the cache (an unusable database is not an error). `NasdaqDirectoryRepository` takes an optional `forget` callable and calls it, then re-raises, when a body fails to parse. Only a `MalformedPayload` evicts: a non-2xx response is never cached, and a good download is kept. The cache stores a 2xx response before the parser sees it, so without this a body that is the wrong thing served with a 200 would be re-served for the full one-hour lifetime and fail every call that needs the tier. That is a real risk here: the directory's host answers through the Imperva CDN (its responses carry `X-CDN: Imperva` and `incap_ses` cookies), and a challenge or error page from such a CDN is typically a 200. Now a bad download costs one failed call. The `EdgarClient` protocol is unchanged (it stays `get`); the hook is passed in by the wiring that builds the directory repository, so no other repository is widened.
- Options considered: keeping the three copies and adding only a test that they agree (rejected: the whitespace-only name needed a code fix anyway, and a test of agreement between copies leaves the copies); accepting the hour-long cache of a bad body and recording it (rejected: the failure is loud but blocks every continued-listing call that needs the directory for up to an hour); adding `forget` to the `EdgarClient` protocol (rejected: five repositories would be widened for one caller); a second protocol checked at run time (rejected: an `isinstance` test on a client is hidden coupling); evicting on every failure (rejected: an upstream failure was never cached, so there is nothing to evict); a stamp-shape check in `DirectoryStamp` (dropped: the text it is compared with is built in the same shape, so no mutation could prove it).
- Why: a citation is only as good as the guarantee that anything the parser lets through can be cited, and a cache that keeps a failed download turns one bad response into an hour of failures.
- Revisit if: a second exchange directory is added (the shapes then need a source), the directory's host stops being a CDN that serves error pages as 200s, or another repository needs to evict on a parse failure (then a shared hook, not a wider protocol, is the next step).

### D43: The Nasdaq symbol directory: strict parser, one-hour lifetime, a third citation kind, a contact-free User-Agent, fixtures by source
- Date: 2026-10-31
- Decision: `ingestion/nasdaq_directory.py` defines `NasdaqDirectoryRepository(client: EdgarClient).fetch() -> NasdaqDirectory`: build the fixed URL with `endpoints.nasdaq_listed_url()` (no argument can reach it), `get` through whatever client it was given, `ensure_success`, parse, and keep the SHA-256 of the exact body bytes. A missing file is an `UpstreamError`, not a not-found: there is no company to be missing. It knows no tier and no status meaning; reading a row as either is the tool layer's job, where it is cited. The host `www.nasdaqtrader.com` joins the allowlist as an exact name (HTTPS, port 443, no trailing dot, as for the SEC hosts), and `ALLOWED_HOSTS` is now the SEC hosts plus that one.
- Parser rules (`ingestion/payloads/nasdaq_directory.py`): the body must decode as UTF-8; lines end in CRLF or LF and the last break is optional; the first line must equal the live header exactly (`Symbol|Security Name|Market Category|Test Issue|Financial Status|Round Lot Size|ETF|NextShares`), never mapped by position, because Nasdaq's definitions page lists fewer columns than the file carries and a renamed, reordered, added or removed column is a changed format; the last line must be the trailer `File Creation Time: mmddyyyyhh:mm` followed by seven empty fields, with a real calendar date and time, and a file without it is refused as cut off (the check that would have caught the truncated fetch that prompted it); every row has exactly eight fields, a Symbol of 1 to 15 characters with no whitespace, a Security Name of 1 to 300 characters with no control character (a trailing space is kept as written), digits for Round Lot Size, and exactly `Y` or `N` for Test Issue, ETF and NextShares; a repeated Symbol, a trailer-looking line before the end and a blank line are refused; and Market Category and Financial Status are one uppercase letter or empty. An unrecognized or blank letter there is carried raw and does not refuse the file, so one odd row cannot break every lookup; what a letter means, and that a matched row with an unrecognized category assesses nothing, is decided where it is used. Every other anomaly refuses the whole file with `MalformedPayload` naming the field or row and never repeating the row's text. The trailer is stored as written (`DirectoryStamp.raw`) beside a naive datetime: Nasdaq states no time zone and none is assumed.
- Lifetime: the directory is cached for one hour (`CacheTtlClass.DIRECTORY`, 3600 seconds, fresh up to and including the hour), because Nasdaq's definitions page says the files are "updated periodically through-out each day" and a status flag should not be a day stale. A 15 minute default would refetch 349 KB more often than the data changes, and 24 hours would let a new deficiency flag lag by a day.
- Citation: a third locator, `DirectoryLocator` (`kind="directory"`), joins the `Citation` union beside `XbrlLocator` and `TextLocator`, which amends D5 (a union of two) and D26 (which fixed the locator list). It carries the file URL, the trailer stamp as written, the SHA-256 of the file's exact bytes, and the matched row's symbol, security name, market category and financial status as raw codes, so a code the product does not know can still be cited. The URL must be on the Nasdaq Trader host and the hash and stamp must have their exact shapes. `NasdaqDirectory.locator(row)` builds it from the validated file and row.
- User-Agent: `EdgarTransport` stamped `ListingLens/0.1.0 (<contact>)` on every request through one client-level header, which would have handed the SEC contact email to a host that is not the SEC. The agent is now chosen per request from the URL's host: the three SEC hosts get the contact, and every other allowlisted host gets `ListingLens/0.1.0` (`branding.public_user_agent`). Nasdaq accepted the contact-free agent (HTTP 200, and gzip when asked, which the transport already decodes). A redirect re-chooses at each hop. Tests prove it at the transport (each SEC host carries the contact; the directory request carries it in no header; redirects in both directions) and on the recorder path through `main(["nasdaq"])`.
- Fixtures: recorded fixtures are organized by source, in one gated recorder run (`record_fixtures.py nasdaq`) on 2026-10-31. The directory file is stored as exact bytes in `tests/fixtures/nasdaq/nasdaqlisted.txt` (348,816 bytes, CRLF kept, SHA-256 `b160a2f2814e5df68d7e414fba70f808f7ad3d20cbbdd167a8a2e38a10044168`) with its own `manifest.json`. Xiao-I's submissions (20,364 bytes) and companyfacts (552,356 bytes) are EDGAR fixtures, new files `submissions_aixi.json` and `companyfacts_aixi.json` in `tests/fixtures/edgar/`, with two entries added to that folder's manifest by the recorder's merge and every existing entry byte-for-byte unchanged. They are untrimmed because `trim_companyfacts` was built to exercise the parser and keeps only the first few concepts, so for this company it drops stockholders' equity, net income, pre-tax income, total assets, revenue and cash (each became `ConceptNotFound` in a trial trim of the live document), which is what the next chunk measures. A manifest test hashes every file in both folders against its entry, and `git check-attr` confirms `.gitattributes` applies `-text` to each, so the CRLF bytes and the hashes survive a checkout.
- The recorded file as it stands: its trailer reads `File Creation Time: 1030202618:01|||||||`, the stamp `1030202618:01` (month 10, day 30, year 2026, 18:01, zone not stated), and it holds 5,616 rows. Its Xiao-I row is `AIXI|XIAO-I Corporation - American Depositary Shares|G|N|D|100|N|N`: Market Category G (Nasdaq Global Market) and Financial Status D (Deficient, "Issuer Failed to Meet Nasdaq Continued Listing Requirements") as of the file stamped 1030202618:01. This replaces D42's undated "financial status D": the status is Nasdaq's flag at that stamp, not a standing property of the company. The SEC lists Xiao-I's ticker as `AIXI` and its exchange as `Nasdaq`, an exact match to the row.
- Options considered: refuse the file on any unrecognized category or status letter (rejected: one odd row would take down every lookup); default an unrecognized category to a tier (rejected: a guess); read the trailer as the definitions page documents it, `mmddyyyyhhmm` (rejected: the live file writes a colon, so a parser true to the page would refuse every real file); parse by column position (rejected: a changed column order would silently read the wrong field); one folder and one manifest for every fixture (rejected: the directory is not EDGAR, and the EDGAR manifest records SEC URLs); trimming the new company's facts, or recording it under a phase-named folder (rejected: the trim loses the concepts and fixtures are organized by source); keeping the single client-level User-Agent and relying on Nasdaq not to log it (rejected: the contact would be disclosed to a non-SEC host); a 15 minute or 24 hour lifetime (see above).
- Why: a tier and a status taken from an exchange file are only worth citing if the file is read exactly as published and fails loudly when it is not, and the contact that SEC's fair-access policy requires belongs to the SEC only.
- Revisit if: Nasdaq changes the header, the trailer or the line endings (the parser refuses the file by design, and a re-record changes the fixture's literal facts on purpose); Nasdaq states a time zone for the stamp; another non-SEC host is allowlisted (it gets the public agent by default); or a second exchange directory is added (the locator then needs a source field).

### D42: Phase 3 architecture: a four-tool stdio MCP server over an in-process ToolService
- Date: 2026-10-31
- Decision: Phase 3 is an MCP server that is a thin adapter over an in-process `ToolService` (D1), on local stdio (D2), with no FastAPI (D17). It exposes four tools and no others: `resolve_company`, `list_filings`, `extract_financials`, `check_standards`. No tool returns filing text, and there are no placeholder tools for capabilities that do not exist yet (pathway, issuer structure, red flags, peers, memo); `get_filing` and full-text search wait for Phase 4's prompt-injection envelope. Packages: `tools/` holds the service, the argument and result models, the findings view and the tier selection, and imports no `mcp`; `server/` is the only package that imports `mcp`; the Nasdaq directory models live in `domain/` and `ingestion/`. Guards in `tests/test_repo_constraints.py` will pin the import boundaries, forbid `httpx2` anywhere in `src`, and forbid `print` under `tools/` and `server/`.
- Arguments: a company is named by exactly one of `cik`, `ticker`, or `name`, three flat optional fields, never one free string that has to be classified by shape (a name such as "NVIDIA" is also ticker-shaped). Every argument is a strict Pydantic model with `extra="forbid"` and bounded string lengths, converted to the domain types (`Cik`, `Ticker`), and a bad argument is refused, never coerced. `check_standards` requires `listing_type` and `pathway` and takes an optional `tier`. No argument can carry a price, market value, holder count, or market-maker count: external inputs wait for a cited price feed, and the external bag stays empty. There is no `as_of` argument: rules in force today are measured against the latest extracted figures, with today taken from an injected clock (the UTC date) so tests stay deterministic. `list_filings` takes an optional list of forms compared case-insensitively and otherwise exactly (no prefix, so `10-K` does not include `10-K/A`; a model sending `10-k` must not get a silent zero), echoes the forms as given, defaults to 20 filings with a hard cap of 100 and no offset, and reports how many matching filings exist beyond those returned.
- Results: every result echoes the resolved company (padded CIK, issuer name, and the reference it was resolved from, per D9) and the date it was evaluated for. The tool layer re-models the domain `FinancialSnapshot` into flat tool models instead of wrapping it: the domain JSON nests `{"cik": {"value": ...}}`, carries accessions as bare 18-digit strings, and its Decimal schema admits a number or a string. Every Decimal leaves as an exact string through one conversion function, and the string pattern accepts everything that function can emit, so a legitimate engine value can never fail output validation and turn into a masked error. A test enumerates each domain model's `model_fields` and fails when a domain field is added without a mapping, so the projection cannot silently fall behind the domain. A company with no XBRL facts is a result with `facts_status` set to `no_xbrl_facts`, not an error: `check_standards` then runs the engine on an empty bag (which reports every concept as absent with no invented gap reason).
- Findings view: `check_standards` returns a view projected from `TierResult`, not `TierResult` itself, in a fixed reading order: the `reported_financials` signal (the engine's financial signal, renamed so it cannot read as vouched for), then the cited findings (every measured rule with its figure citation, rule citation, verified flag and translation notes), then per-standard and per-branch states, then what could not be measured and why, and last the verdict with its caveat. This follows D41's findings that a continued-listing verdict is usually `cannot_determine` from EDGAR facts alone and that a multi-year branch keeps a standard unknown even when the latest year fails. The view copies the engine's states, margins and verdicts exactly and never recomputes them. Every rule result appears exactly once, in the bucket its state dictates: a validator on the view enforces that structure, and an exhaustive test over every recorded company and rule set checks it against the engine with its own bucket mapping. One fixed scope note says the figures are as reported in SEC XBRL filings, are not verified by ListingLens, and are a risk signal and not investment advice.
- Tier selection: the tier assessed is either the one the caller names or the one Nasdaq's own symbol directory cites, never a guess. The directory lookup always runs and its outcome is always reported. Initial listing with no tier named assesses all three tiers, because a company still registering is choosing one. A named tier assesses that tier only, with the directory outcome still reported so a mismatch is visible. Continued listing with no tier named matches each SEC ticker exactly against the directory's Symbol column, with test issues never matching and no normalization beyond what both sources document: one shared category assesses that tier, several categories assess each, labeled by ticker, and no match or no ticker assesses nothing and reports what was tried (continued-listing standards apply only to listed companies; a caller may name a tier for a what-if). A matched row with a blank or unrecognized category assesses nothing and reports the raw code. If the directory cannot be read, a continued-listing call with no tier named and at least one ticker needs it and fails with the typed message; every other combination proceeds, reports that the directory was unavailable with the error's message, and returns no Nasdaq status. A failure is always visible, and supplementary evidence failing never discards an assessment that did not depend on it. Initial listing is encoded for `traditional_ipo` only, so any other pathway honestly returns "no rule in force", never a pass.
- Nasdaq directory: the source is the HTTPS file `www.nasdaqtrader.com/dynamic/SymDir/nasdaqlisted.txt` (the FTP copy is not used), fetched through the existing cached client with the host added to the allowlist and a one hour cache lifetime, since Nasdaq says the files are updated throughout each day. Parsing matches the live header exactly and fails loudly on any change, a missing trailer (the truncation guard) or any malformed row; the one tolerated variation is a blank or unrecognized single category or status code, which is carried raw so one odd row cannot break every lookup, and a matched row is never guessed at. Observed live on 2026-10-31: the trailer is `File Creation Time: 1030202618:01` followed by seven empty fields, so the stamp is `mmddyyyyhh:mm` with a colon where Nasdaq's definitions page says `mmddyyyyhhmm`, lines end in CRLF, and no time zone is stated, so the stamp is recorded as given and no zone is assumed. Every tier determination cites the file through a third citation kind (URL, creation stamp, file hash, and the matched row's symbol, category and status), which joins the `Citation` union and so amends D5 (and D26, which fixed the locator list). Nasdaq's financial status is reported beside our assessment with its meaning and a citation; it is the exchange's flag, not a ListingLens finding, and it never feeds the engine or the verdict. Requests to nasdaqtrader.com carry a contact-free User-Agent: the transport currently stamps the SEC contact email on every request, which would hand it to a non-SEC host.
- Errors and secrets: argument conversion in the tools layer raises its own typed error (a subclass of `InputValidationError`), and only that error maps to a readable tool error; an `InputValidationError` raised after the arguments are accepted comes from an engine model refusing an internal invariant (such as `TierResult` refusing a forged value) and is masked like any other internal failure, because presenting it as the caller's bad input would be false. Typed ingestion errors map to a tool error with their own message, and an ambiguous company returns its candidates so an agent can retry with a CIK. Configuration and standards errors and every other exception stay masked and are logged to stderr. The SEC contact lives only in the User-Agent header, never in a message. The server makes no LLM call and needs no Anthropic key. Filer-supplied strings in results (entity and security names, form types, document names) are data, and the tool descriptions say so.
- Process model: the SQLite cache connection is bound to its creating thread and the request throttle is not thread-safe, and the SDK runs a plain `def` tool in a worker thread. So every tool is `async def` and calls the synchronous service directly on the event-loop thread, and the service is built and closed in the server lifespan on that same thread, one call at a time. A test fires concurrent calls through the in-process client and records the thread of each, and dispatching to a thread pool must fail it. The cost is that a call blocks the loop, including throttle and retry sleeps, so a host cancel or ping waits until it ends. Stdout is the protocol wire: nothing prints, logs go to stderr, and a real-stdio subprocess test proves `tools/list` round-trips without touching the network. Configuration is explicit, because a host can launch the server from any directory: `SEC_EDGAR_CONTACT` and an absolute `LISTINGLENS_CACHE_DIR` are required (a missing contact fails at startup with a readable stderr message), an optional `LISTINGLENS_LOG_LEVEL` exists, and the server never reads a `.env` from the current directory.
- Fixtures and testing: recorded fixtures are organized by source. The new Nasdaq company's submissions and companyfacts are EDGAR fixtures, recorded untrimmed (the recorder's trim drops the concepts extraction needs for it) as new files in `tests/fixtures/edgar/` with additions-only manifest entries, and the directory file is stored as exact bytes under `tests/fixtures/nasdaq/` with its own manifest, all in one recorder run. The chosen company is Xiao-I (AIXI): US-GAAP, filed on 20-F, Nasdaq Global Market, financial status D, with all figures from FY2025. Tests use hand-written literal tables, mocked transports and the socket guard; nothing makes a real call or costs money.
- Known findings from live data (recorded here, not fixed in Phase 3's first four commits): first, ATA Creativity's income from continuing operations resolves to a FY2021 figure while every other figure is FY2025, because the primary tag stopped being used and nothing checks recency, so the engine reports a four-year-old figure as a latest-year breach. This is a defect, Phase 3 does not close until it is fixed, and it gets its own plan pass and fix commit after the live smoke so the smoke can surface sibling staleness cases first. Second, AGM Group's figures all cite a `POS AM` filing with no fiscal-period label although its 20-F figures exist, so year-end rules read not evaluable where a measurement was possible; this is the prefer-annual tie-break that D31 deferred, now observed on real data, and it is triaged after the smoke. `tests/known_failing.txt` does not change, and the live smoke adds both companies as witnesses.
- Options considered: one free-text company argument (rejected: shape-based guessing); wrapping the domain snapshot (rejected: nested wrappers, bare accessions, ambiguous Decimal schema); caller-supplied market inputs or an `as_of` argument (rejected: unciteable inputs and a second source of truth for the date); inferring the tier from a name or a fuzzy ticker match (rejected: a guess); mapping every `InputValidationError` to a tool error (rejected: it would blame the caller for an internal break); synchronous tools on the SDK's thread pool, a lock around the service, or a dedicated worker thread (rejected for now: the pool breaks the thread-bound SQLite connection, a lock cannot fix thread binding, and a worker thread is more code than a single-client stdio server needs); a 15 minute or 24 hour directory lifetime (rejected: more fetches than needed, or a stale deficiency flag); recording the new company trimmed or into a phase-named directory (rejected: the trim loses the target concepts, and fixtures are organized by source).
- Why: the product is a measured risk signal with every claim cited, so the tool layer must show what was measured and why before it states a verdict, must never present what it could not measure as a pass or fail, and must keep the exchange's own flag separate from its own findings.
- Revisit if: a remote transport, a second concurrent client, or a host timeout or stall during the live smoke appears (move the service onto a dedicated owning thread); a cited price feed or a multi-year series arrives (the external bag and the continued-listing verdicts change); Nasdaq changes the directory format (the parser fails loudly by design); or the recency fix for the first finding changes which figures a latest-year rule measures.

### D41: The margin engine on real data: Trans American, Luckin, and GE through the whole Phase 2 path
- Date: 2026-10-31
- Decision: for the first time the whole Phase 2 path runs on real data in one test. A recorded companyfacts fixture is served by the real transport stack (a URL-routed mock under the real cache and repository) to the real `ExtractionService.extract_snapshot`, then `Inputs.from_snapshot`, then `assess` against the real ruleset, giving a `TierResult`.
